An Additive-Noise Approximation to Keller–Segel–Dean–Kawasaki Dynamics: Small-Noise Results
Adrian Martini, Avi Mayorcas

Abstract
We study an additive-noise approximation to Keller–Segel–Dean–Kawasaki dynamics, which is proposed as an approximate model to the fluctuating hydrodynamics of chemotactically interacting particles around their mean-field limit. As such, the interaction potential is given by the Green’s function associated to Poisson’s equation, which is singular around the origin. Two parameters play a key rôle in the approximation: the noise intensity ε \varepsilon which captures the amplitude of fluctuations (tending to zero as the effective system size tends to infinity) and the correlation length δ \delta which represents the effective scale under consideration. Let δ ⁡ ( ε ) → 0 \delta(\varepsilon)\to 0 as ε → 0 \varepsilon\to 0 . Under the relative scaling assumption lim ε → 0 ε ​ log ⁡ ( δ ​ ( ε ) − 1 ) = 0 \lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})=0 we obtain analogues of law of large numbers and large deviation principles in irregular spaces of distributions using methods of singular stochastic partial differential equations. The same techniques also yield a central limit theorem under the relative scaling lim ε → 0 ε 1 / 2 ​ log ⁡ ( δ ​ ( ε ) − 1 ) = 0 \lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})=0 . Assuming the more restrictive relative scaling lim ε → 0 ε 1 / 2 ​ δ − γ − 2 = 0 \lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta^{-\gamma-2}=0 for some γ ∈ ( − 1 / 2 , 0 ) \gamma\in(-1/2,0) , we also obtain analogues of law of large numbers and large deviation principles in regular function spaces using a mixture of pathwise and probabilistic tools. We further describe consequences of these results relevant to applications of our approximation in studying continuum fluctuations of particle systems.



Keywords
: fluctuating hydrodynamics, Dean–Kawasaki equation, Keller–Segel equation, law of large numbers, central limit theorem, large deviation principle, singular stochastic partial differential equation;

MSC2020
: Primary: 82C31, 60H17. Secondary: 60L40, 60H15, 60F05, 60F10, 92C17.

1
Introduction

The goal of this work is to understand the behaviour of the so-called
Keller–Segel–Dean–Kawasaki
equation,

\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} (\partial_{t}-\Delta)\rho&=-\chi\nabla\cdot(\rho\nabla\Phi_{\rho})-\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\nabla\cdot(\sqrt{\rho}\bm{\xi}),&&\penalty\ \text{in}\penalty\ (0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\\
-\Delta\Phi_{\rho}&=\rho-\langle\rho,1\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})},&&\penalty\ \text{in}\penalty\ (0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\\
\rho|_{t=0}&=\rho_{0},&&\penalty\ \text{on}\penalty\ \mathbb{T}^{d},\end{aligned}\end{cases}

(1.1)

defined on the
d\in\mathbb{N}
-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,
where
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
is the
chemotactic sensitivity
,

\bm{\xi}
denotes a vector-valued space-time white noise
and
\varepsilon>0
the
noise intensity
.
Assume that the initial data
\rho_{0}
is non-negative and of unit mass.

The interest in (
1.1
) stems from its connection to the system of weakly-interacting overdamped Langevin diffusions,

\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muX^{i}_{t}=\sqrt{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muB^{i}_{t}+\frac{\chi}{N}\sum_{j=1,\,j\neq i}^{N}\nabla\mathscr{G}(X^{i}_{t}-X^{j}_{t})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mut,\qquad i=1,\ldots,N,

(1.2)

where
N\in\mathbb{N}
denotes the population size,

(B^{i})_{i=1}^{N}
a family of independent standard Brownian motions on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
and
\mathscr{G}
the Green’s function associated to Poisson’s equation on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
(so that
\Phi_{\rho}=\mathscr{G}\ast\rho
.)
For every
x\in[-1/2,1/2]^{d}
one can decompose
\mathscr{G}([x])=K(x)+K_{0}(x)
, where
[x]=x+\mathbb{Z}^{2}
denotes the associated element of
\mathbb{T}^{d}
,
K
the Green’s function associated to Poisson’s equation on the full space and
K_{0}
a smooth correction to periodize
\mathscr{G}
.
In particular, it follows that
\mathscr{G}
is singular around the origin.
This combined with the singularity of
\Delta\mathscr{G}
causes the dynamics of (
1.2
) to be highly non-trivial, see for example
[
FT25
]
.

Passing to the limit
N\to\infty
, one may expect the system (
1.2
) to be a stochastic particle approximation to the solution
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
of the deterministic (parabolic-elliptic)
(Patlak–)Keller–Segel
PDE of chemotaxis
[
Pat53
,
KS70
]
,

\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} (\partial_{t}-\Delta)\rho_{\textnormal{det}}&=-\chi\nabla\cdot(\rho_{\textnormal{det}}\nabla\Phi_{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}),&&\penalty\ \text{in}\penalty\ (0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\\
-\Delta\Phi_{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}&=\rho_{\textnormal{det}}-\langle\rho_{\textnormal{det}},1\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})},&&\penalty\ \text{in}\penalty\ (0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\\
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}|_{t=0}&=\rho_{0},&&\penalty\ \text{on}\penalty\ \mathbb{T}^{d}.\end{aligned}\end{cases}

(1.3)

This convergence has been verified in some cases depending on domain, dimension and
\chi
, see e.g.
[
FJ17
,
Tar24
,
BJW23
]
.

On the other hand, for
\varepsilon=2/N
, equation (
1.1
) describes the continuum fluctuations of (
1.2
) through the theory of fluctuating hydrodynamics (FHD)
[
Dea96
,
Kaw94
,
LL87
,
Spo91
,
GLP99
,
BGN16
]
, which is concerned with random fluctuations of large systems around their averaged behaviour as well as characterizing rare events of these systems. Large deviation principles (LDP) and central limit theorems (CLT) are concrete mathematical examples of such statements. Results of this type can often be leveraged to study phenomenological properties of the associated systems such as metastability, non-equilibrium phase transitions and long-range correlations,
[
Ber+15
]
. The theory is also related to robust simulation of large, random systems or those exhibiting small-scale unpredictability.

However, despite the numerous motivations to study (
1.1
), it provides significant analytic challenges. Not only is it supercritical in the sense of
[
Hai14
]
, it was also shown by Konarovskyi, Lehmann and von Renesse
[
KLvR19
,
KLvR20
]
that stochastic PDEs driven by
Dean–Kawasaki noise

\nabla\cdot(\sqrt{\rho}\bm{\xi})
are unstable. Indeed upon replacing
\mathscr{G}
by a smooth function, it has been shown in
[
KLvR20
]
, that a solution (in the sense of a martingale problem) exists if and only if the initial data is an empirical measure and
\varepsilon=2/N
. Even though there are singular interactions that recover well-posedness (see e.g. the references in
[
KLvR20
]
), we do not expect the situation to be improved in the case of (
1.1
).

In recent years there has been great progress in finding good approximations to equations driven by Dean–Kawasaki noise
[
FG23
,
DFG20
,
WWZ24
,
WZ24
,
CF23
,
CS23
,
Cor+26
,
CF25
,
DKP24
,
DJP25
]
.
However, the case of interactions that are both non-incompressible and singular beyond the Ladyzhenskaya–Prodi–Serrin condition, such as
\nabla\mathscr{G}
, is still open.

Inspired by the physics literature
[
Mah+21
]
, we take a first step in this direction by considering an
additive-noise approximation

\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta}
given by

\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} (\partial_{t}-\Delta)\rho&=-\chi\nabla\cdot(\rho\nabla\Phi_{\rho})-\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\nabla\cdot(\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\bm{\xi}^{\delta}),&&\penalty\ \text{in}\penalty\ (0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\\
-\Delta\Phi_{\rho}&=\rho-\langle\rho,1\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})},&&\penalty\ \text{in}\penalty\ (0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\\
\rho|_{t=0}&=\rho_{0},&&\penalty\ \text{on}\penalty\ \mathbb{T}^{d},\end{aligned}\end{cases}

(1.4)

where
\delta>0
denotes the
correlation length
,

\psi_{\delta}
a smooth spatial mollifier of scale
\delta
,

\bm{\xi}^{\delta}\coloneq\psi_{\delta}\ast\bm{\xi}

and
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
the solution to the deterministic Keller–Segel equation (
1.3
).

While the additive-noise structure of (
1.4
) does not satisfy a natural fluctuation-dissipation relation, i.e. it is no longer a gradient flow, we advocate it for two reasons. Firstly, as we will show in this paper, it provides the correct Gaussian fluctuations around the mean-field limit and secondly, at the time of writing, it has not been possible to incorporate truly non-linear noise, in the style of
[
FG24
,
FG23
,
WWZ24
,
WZ24
]
, along with singular non-local interactions beyond the Ladyzhenskaya–Prodi–Serrin threshold.

1.1
Main Results

In the following, due to reasons of scaling sub-criticality
[
Hai14
]
, we restrict our attention to two dimensions. In this situation, it is known for large
\chi
that the solution
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
to the deterministic Keller–Segel equation can blow up in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
in finite time, see e.g.
[
KX16
, Thm. 8.1]
. Denote this deterministic blow-up time by
T^{*}
and choose the time horizon
T<T^{*}
, so that the additive noise of (
1.4
) is well-defined on
[0,T]
.
Let
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be the space of Hölder–Besov distributions of regularity
\alpha+1<-1
(for the definition see Subsection
1.3
.) By the regularity of space-time white noise in two dimensions, it follows that
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
is the natural regularity of solutions to (
1.4
).
However, due to the presence of the stochastic term in (
1.4
), it may still happen that
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
blows up in
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
before time
T
. Hence, we shall work in a space
(\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
of functions continuous in time and taking values in
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
that are allowed to blow up before time
T
but once blown up are not allowed to return. For the precise details, see Subsection
1.3
. We further denote by
\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
the Sobolev space of regularity
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
. The space
(\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
is then defined
mutatis mutandis
.

Sketch of Theorem
4.4

(LLN)
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\langle\rho_{0},1\rangle_{L^{2}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
1.3
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence. Assume
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Then for all
T<T^{*}
and
\alpha\in(-9/4,-2)
, it follows that
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}\to\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
in
(\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
in probability as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Sketch of Proof.

Passing to the paracontrolled decomposition
[
GIP15
]
, we can show that the solution map is locally Lipschitz continuous in a vector of stochastic objects
\mathbb{X}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
known as the noise enhancement. Using the relative scaling
\varepsilon\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})\to 0
, we can deduce that
\mathbb{X}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}\to 0
in probability as
\varepsilon\to 0
. The claim then follows by the continuous mapping theorem. For the full proof see Theorem
4.4
.
∎

Theorem
4.4
is consistent with the propagation of chaos for (
1.2
) which was shown to converge to the mean-field limit (
1.3
) for appropriate initial data and
\chi
sufficiently small, see
[
FJ17
,
Tar24
,
BJW19a
,
BJW19
,
BJW23
,
CRS25
]
.

The same continuities of the solution map also allow us to establish a CLT.

Sketch of Theorem
4.6

(CLT)
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\langle\rho_{0},1\rangle_{L^{2}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
1.3
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence. Assume
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Then for all
T<T^{*}
and
\alpha\in(-9/4,-2)
, it follows that
\varepsilon^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}-\rho_{\textnormal{det}})\to v
in
(\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
in probability as
\varepsilon\to 0
, where
v\in C_{T}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
is the unique mild solution to

\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} (\partial_{t}-\Delta)v&=-\chi\nabla\cdot(v\nabla\Phi_{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}})-\chi\nabla\cdot(\rho_{\textnormal{det}}\nabla\Phi_{v})-\nabla\cdot(\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\bm{\xi}),\\
v|_{t=0}&=0.\end{aligned}\end{cases}

(1.5)

We refer to this
v
as the generalized Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process.

Proof.

For a proof see Theorem
4.6
.
∎

In the repulsive case (
\chi<0
) the CLT for the particle system (
1.2
) has been shown in
[
CN25
]
with leading-order fluctuations converging to the generalized Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process (
1.5
). In the attractive case (
\chi>0
), a CLT for (
1.2
) is to our knowledge still open. However, if (
1.2
) allows a CLT (possibly for a certain range of small
\chi
), we would expect the leading-order fluctuations to converge to (
1.5
).
See also
[
WZZ23
]
for a CLT of the point-vortex approximation to the two-dimensional incompressible Navier–Stokes equation with leading-order fluctuations converging to a generalized Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process driven by
\nabla^{\perp}\Phi
.
Consequently, the combination of Theorems
4.4
&
4.6
leads us to expect that our additive-noise approximation is precise up to first order.

Another first-order approximation is given by
linear fluctuating hydrodynamics

\rho_{\textnormal{det}}+\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}v
, which, however, is not able to model noise-induced aggregation when started from uniform initial data
\rho_{0}\equiv 1
. On the other hand, it is clear that (
1.4
) can blow up to a Dirac mass due to the fluctuations interacting with the non-linearity, even when started from uniformity.

Finally, we can also show an LDP by adapting the techniques of
[
FV07
,
HW15
]
.

Sketch of Theorem
4.8

(LDP)
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\langle\rho_{0},1\rangle_{L^{2}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
1.3
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence. Assume
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Then for all
T<T^{*}
and
\alpha\in(-9/4,-2)
, it follows that
(\rho_{\delta(\varepsilon)}^{(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
satisfies an LDP in
(\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
with speed
\varepsilon
and good rate function

\mathscr{I}(\rho)\coloneq\inf\Bigl\{\frac{1}{2}\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}:(\partial_{t}-\Delta)\rho=-\chi\nabla\cdot(\rho\nabla\Phi_{\rho})-\nabla\cdot(\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}h),\penalty\ \rho|_{t=0}=\rho_{0}\Bigr\}.

(1.6)

Sketch of Proof.

An adaptation of
[
HW15
]
combined with the relative scaling allows us to deduce an LDP for the noise enhancement
(\mathbb{X}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
, which we can then transfer to
(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
via the contraction principle. For the full proof see Theorem
4.8
.
∎

Remark 1.1
.

Results similar to Theorem
4.8
were also established in
[
CD19
,
CD19a
]
for both the
\Phi^{2n}_{d}
equation and the two-dimensional stochastic Navier–Stokes equation by combining careful energy estimates with the weak-convergence approach. We do not rule out the existence of another proof for Theorem
4.8
based on these techniques, but do not pursue this line of enquiry any further in this paper.

The LDP for the particle system (
1.2
) is to our knowledge still open. In the case of Lipschitz interactions, a first result was given by
[
DG87
]
, see also
[
Har+18
,
Cog+20
,
Hoe+24
,
CG25
]
for LDPs under more general assumptions on the regularity of the interaction.

After formal manipulations, using the idea that the
Dean–Kawasaki noise

\nabla\cdot(\sqrt{\rho}\bm{\xi})
appearing in (
1.1
) is
\delta
-correlated with respect to the local metric tensor of the Otto–Wasserstein metric
[
Ott01
]
, one can bring the rate function of
[
DG87
]
into the following form:

\mathscr{A}(\rho)\coloneq\inf\Bigl\{\frac{1}{2}\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}:(\partial_{t}-\Delta)\rho=-\chi\nabla\cdot(\rho\nabla\Phi_{\rho})-\nabla\cdot(\sqrt{\rho}h),\penalty\ \rho|_{t=0}=\rho_{0}\Bigr\}.

(1.7)

Comparing (
1.6
) and (
1.7
), we find that the approximation error lies in the so-called
skeleton equation
appearing inside the infimum. The additive-noise approximation (
1.4
) induces a skeleton that is additive in the Cameron–Martin element
h
, whereas the interacting particle system (
1.2
) is associated to a fully nonlinear skeleton.

Let
\alpha\in(-9/4,-2)
and denote by

the blow-up time of
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
in
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
. In the next result we establish that the probability of observing a blow-up in
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
before time
T<T^{*}
is exponentially small in
\varepsilon^{-1}
.

Sketch of Theorem
4.12
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\langle\rho_{0},1\rangle_{L^{2}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
1.3
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence. Assume
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Then for all
S\leq T<T^{*}
and
\alpha\in(-9/4,-2)
, there exists a constant
c>0
such that

and the continuity of the skeleton equation in the Cameron–Martin element. For the full proof see Theorem
4.12
.
∎

While Theorem
4.12
rules out the possibility of observig a blow-up with high probability, it can still occur that (
1.4
) produces negative values, which is a major modelling error of the additive-noise approximation. To control this event, we impose a more restrictive relative scaling,
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta(\varepsilon)^{-2}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
We denote the negative part of every
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
by
u^{-}\coloneq\min\{0,u\}
and control
(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})^{-}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
.

Sketch of Theorem
3.6
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\langle\rho_{0},1\rangle_{L^{2}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
1.3
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence. Assume
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta(\varepsilon)^{-2}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Then for all
T<T^{*}
,
L>0
and
\lambda>0
, there exists some
\mu>0
such that

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon(1+\delta(\varepsilon)^{-2})^{2}\log\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\lVert(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})^{-}\rVert_{C_{S_{L}}L^{2}}\geq\lambda\Bigr)\lesssim-\mu^{2}\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\rVert_{C_{T}L^{2}\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}}^{-2},

where

S_{L}\coloneq T\wedge L\wedge\inf\Bigl\{t\in[0,T]:\lVert\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}(t)\rVert_{L^{2}}>L\Bigr\}

denotes the first time that the
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
-norm of
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
exceeds the level
L
.

Sketch of Proof.

We first pass to the mild formulation of (
1.4
) given by

\rho=P\rho_{0}-\chi\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[\rho\nabla\Phi_{\rho}]-\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta},

(1.8)

where
P_{t}\coloneq\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{t\Delta}
denotes the heat semigroup,
\mathcal{I}[f]_{t}\coloneq\int_{0}^{t}P_{t-s}f_{s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus
the resolution of the heat equation and
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}\coloneq\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\bm{\xi}^{\delta}]
the (additive) stochastic heat equation.
The bound then follows by an infinite-dimensional sub-Gaussian upper deviation inequality on
\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\rVert_{C_{T}L^{2}\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}}
(see Lemma
E.5
), combined with the continuity of
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
in
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}
and the continuity of
u\mapsto u^{-}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
. For the full proof see Theorem
3.6
.
∎

Under the same restrictive relative scaling, one can also show an LLN & LDP in spaces of higher regularity.

Sketch of Theorem
3.3

(LLN)
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\langle\rho_{0},1\rangle_{L^{2}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
1.3
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence. Assume
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta(\varepsilon)^{-\gamma-2}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
for some
\gamma\in(-1/2,0]
. Then for all
T<T^{*}
, it follows that
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}\to\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
in
(\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
in probability as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Sketch of Proof.

Using the relative scaling of
(\varepsilon,\delta(\varepsilon))
, we can deduce that
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\to 0
in
C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
in probability as
\varepsilon\to 0
. The claim then follows by the continuous mapping theorem and the continuity of
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
in
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}
. For the full proof see Theorem
3.3
.
∎

Sketch of Theorem
3.5

(LDP)
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\langle\rho_{0},1\rangle_{L^{2}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
1.3
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence. Assume
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta(\varepsilon)^{-\gamma-2}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
for some
\gamma\in(-1/2,0)
. Then for all
T<T^{*}
, it follows that the sequence
(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
satisfies an LDP in
(\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
with speed
\varepsilon
and good rate function
\mathscr{I}
.

Sketch of Proof.

An application of the weak-convergence approach to large deviations, the Boué–Dupuis formula
[
BDM08
]
and the relative scaling yields an LDP for
(\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
, which we can then transfer to
(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
via the contraction principle. For the full proof see Theorem
3.5
.
∎

In the present setting there is no analogue of the CLT (Theorem
4.6
) since the leading-order fluctuations
\varepsilon^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}-\rho_{\textnormal{det}})
are driven by
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}
, which does not converge in spaces
\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
of regularity
\gamma>-1/2
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Remark 1.2

(Extensions to More General Non-Local Interactions)
.

We naturally expect many of the the arguments of the current paper and
[
MM25
]
to generalize beyond the two-dimensional (attractive and repulsive) Coulomb potentials considered herein.
Without detailing all possible variants of the theorems above, we highlight that the two main features we appeal to are sub-criticality and symmetries of our interaction. Concerning the former property, if one considers a more general interaction
C_{T}\mathcal{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\ni\rho\mapsto\nabla\Psi_{\rho}\in\mathcal{C}^{\beta+\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
bounded and linear, for
\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, one would be required to check that the product
\rho\nabla\Psi_{\rho}
is either classically well-defined or
sub-critical
in the sense of singular SPDE, i.e. that it can be expected to have
homogeneity
lower than that of almost sure realisations of the noise. On the other hand, symmetry of the Coulomb potential plays a rôle for us in showing that the required renormalisation fields diverge less quickly than one would expect from simple power counting.
A similar phenomenon is observed in stochastic fluid equations, see for example
[
DD02
]
.

1.2
Discussion of Relative Scalings

The more we make use of the mollification in (
1.4
), the more regularity we can assume of the noise
\bm{\xi}^{\delta}
at the price of a faster divergence as
\delta\to 0
, which in turn one then needs to compensate with the noise intensity
\varepsilon
.
Hence, throughout this paper we consider different relative scalings between
\varepsilon
and
\delta
.
The general relative scaling assumes
\varepsilon\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})\to 0
and applies in Hölder–Besov spaces
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
for all
\alpha\in(-9/4,-2)
,
whereas the restrictive relative scaling imposes that
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta(\varepsilon)^{-\gamma-2}\to 0
as
\varepsilon,\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
for some
\gamma\in(-1/2,0]
and applies in the Sobolev space
\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
.
Theorems
4.4
,
4.6
,
4.8
and
4.12
rely on the general relative scaling and are collectively referred to as the
rough setting
, since they make minimal use of the mollifier in (
1.4
).
Theorems
3.3
,
3.5
and
3.6
rely on the restrictive relative scaling and are collectively referred to as the
regular setting
. See Table
1
for a summary.

Name

Scaling

Regularity

Results

General

\varepsilon\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})\to 0

\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})

Thms.
4.4
,
4.6
,
4.8
&
4.12

as
\varepsilon,\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0

\alpha\in(-9/4,-2)

(rough setting, Sec.
4
)

Restrictive

\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta(\varepsilon)^{-\gamma-2}\to 0

\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})

Thms.
3.3
,
3.5
&
3.6

as
\varepsilon,\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0

\gamma\in(-1/2,0]

(regular setting, Sec.
3
)

Table 1:
Summary of the different relative scalings used throughout this paper.

Given the motivation from fluctuating hydrodynamics (see (
1.2
)) one natural scaling relation to hope for is based on the intra-particle distance between uniformly distributed particles. If it were to hold that at each
t\geq 0
the
(X^{i}_{t})_{i=1}^{N}
were uniformly i.i.d. distributed (or indeed uniformly separated e.g. on a lattice) then the average intra-particle distance in two dimensions is proportional to
N^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
(more generally
N^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{d}}}
). Hence, the natural scaling to aim for in this setting would be

\delta(N)=N^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}},\quad\varepsilon(N)=2/N\quad\implies\quad\lim_{N\to\infty}\varepsilon(N)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta(N)^{-1}=\sqrt{2}.

(1.9)

On the other hand, for general interactions there is no reason to expect particles to be either uniformly distributed or uniformly separated at positive times. To wit in the case of the Keller–Segel particle system, for
\chi>8\pi
one expects all particles to concentrate at one location at some random time, see
[
FT25
]
. The heuristic (
1.9
) is more naturally applicable to particle systems set on a lattice such as exclusion and zero-range processes
[
DFG20
,
FG23
]

or for interacting diffusions with mutually repulsive potential
[
Ser23
,
Ser18
,
Ser20
]
.

While our restrictive scaling regime,
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta(\varepsilon)^{-\gamma-2}=0
, and the natural scaling, (
1.9
), are mutually exclusive,
the general scaling,
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})=0
, is more permissive and always allows for (
1.9
).

Structure of the Paper:

In the rest of this section (Subsection
1.3
) we introduce notations and conventions used throughout the paper.
In Section
2
we establish the existence and regularity of the deterministic, periodic Keller–Segel equation.
In Section
3
we prove the LLN in the regular setting (Theorem
3.3
). In the same section we also obtain the LDP in the regular setting (Theorem
3.5
). As an application of the theory developed in Section
3
we prove in Subsection
3.4
that solutions to our additive-noise approximation become negative with exponentially small probability (Theorem
3.6
).
In Section
4
we prove the LLN in the rough setting (Theorem
4.4
). In the same section we also obtain a CLT (Theorem
4.6
) and the LDP in the rough setting (Theorem
4.8
). As an application of the theory developed in Section
4
we prove in Subsection
4.5
that solutions to our additive-noise approximation blow up with exponentially small probability (Theorem
4.12
).
Finally, we collect a number of necessary results in a handful of appendices. Appendix
A
recaps properties of various function spaces. Appendix
B
covers the definition and necessary properties of periodic space-time white noise. Appendices
C
,
D
and
E
cover necessary extensions of Friedlin–Wentzell large deviations theory to noise enhancements, analysis of the noise enhancement driven by a Cameron–Martin element and regularity of solutions to the additive stochastic heat equation relevant in our case.

Acknowledgements:

1.3
Notations and Conventions

We write
\mathbb{N}
for the natural numbers excluding zero,
\mathbb{N}_{0}\coloneq\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and
\mathbb{N}_{-1}\coloneq\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{-1\}
. We define the two-dimensional torus by
\mathbb{T}^{2}\coloneq\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Throughout,
\lvert\,\cdot\,\rvert
will indicate the norm
\lvert x\rvert=\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{2}\lvert x_{i}\rvert^{2}\bigr)^{1/2}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
or the induced metric on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
, depending on the context.
For
r>0
we use the notation
B(0,r)\coloneq\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\lvert x\rvert<r\}
. From now on we will write
\langle a,b\rangle
to denote the inner product on any Hilbert space which we either specify or leave clear from the context. For
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
we denote by
C^{k}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{n})
(resp.
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{n})
) the space of
k
-times continuously differentiable (resp. smooth) functions on the torus with values in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We write
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})
for the dual of
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{n})
for vectors of elements in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})
. Denote by
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\textnormal{per}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{n})
the space of periodic distributions and (with an abuse of notation)
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\textnormal{per}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{n})
the space of tempered distributions that are periodic in the space variable. Let
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{n})
be the space of tempered distributions on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
.

For
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})
(resp. complex sequences
(\zeta(\omega))_{\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
with
\overline{\zeta(-\omega)}=\zeta(\omega)
that decay faster than any polynomial) we define its Fourier transform (resp. inverse Fourier transform) by the expression,

\mathscr{F}f(\omega)\coloneq\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 0.7mu\langle\omega,x\rangle}f(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mux,\qquad\mathscr{F}^{-1}\zeta(x)\coloneq\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 0.7mu\langle\omega,x\rangle}\zeta(\omega).

This is extended componentwise to vector-valued functions, by density to
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{n})
for
p\in[1,\infty)
and by duality to
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Where convenient we use the shorthand
\widehat{f}(\omega)\coloneq\mathscr{F}f(\omega)
.

We define the Bessel potential space
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{n})
of regularity
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
as the space of periodic distributions
u\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that

\lVert u\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}}\coloneq\Bigl(\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}(1+\lvert 2\pi\omega\rvert^{2})^{\alpha}\lvert\widehat{u}(\omega)\rvert^{2}\Bigr)^{1/2}<\infty.

(1.10)

We denote the mean by
\overline{u}\coloneq\langle u,1\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}
for each
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})=\mathcal{H}^{0}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})
.

We often work in the scale of Besov and Hölder–Besov spaces whose definitions we summarize. For their basic properties, see e.g.
[
MM25
, App. A]
or the references therein. We let:

\begin{split}&\varrho_{-1},\varrho_{0}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2};[0,1])\penalty\ \text{be radially symmetric and such that}\\
&\textnormal{supp}(\varrho_{-1})\subset B(0,1/2),\qquad\textnormal{supp}(\varrho_{0})\subset\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:9/32\leq\lvert x\rvert\leq 1\},\\
&\sum_{k=-1}^{\infty}\varrho_{k}(x)=1\quad\text{for all}\penalty\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\quad\text{where}\penalty\ \varrho_{k}(x)\coloneq\varrho_{0}(2^{-k}x)\quad\text{for each}\penalty\ k\in\mathbb{N}.\end{split}

This defines a dyadic partition of unity. Given
k\geq-1
we write
\Delta_{k}u\coloneq\mathscr{F}^{-1}(\varrho_{k}\mathscr{F}u)
for the associated Littlewood–Paley block and given
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
p,q\in[1,\infty]
, we define the Besov-norm
\lVert u\rVert_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\coloneq\lVert(2^{k\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{k}u\rVert_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{n})})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{-1}}\rVert_{\ell^{q}}
for
u\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We use
\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{n})
to denote the completion of
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{n})
under
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{p,q}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
and the shorthand
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{n})\coloneq\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The space
\mathcal{B}_{2,2}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{n})
coincides with
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
For
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
p,q\in[1,\infty]
we use the notation
\mathcal{B}^{\alpha-}_{p,q}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\coloneq\cap_{\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha}\mathcal{B}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
. When the context is clear we will remove the target space so as to lighten notation.

For
u,v\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})
we define the paraproduct
\mathbin{\varolessthan}
and resonant product
\mathbin{\varodot}
by

u\mathbin{\varolessthan}v\coloneq\sum_{k\geq-1}\sum_{l=-1}^{k-2}\Delta_{l}u\Delta_{k}v,\qquad u\mathbin{\varodot}v\coloneq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{-1}\\
\lvert k-l\rvert\leq 1\end{subarray}}\Delta_{l}u\Delta_{k}v

and extend them to the scale of Besov distributions by Bony’s lemma
[
BCD11
, Thm. 2.82 & Thm. 2.85]
and generalizations thereof.

We define the action of the heat semigroup on
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})
by the flow,

[0,\infty)\ni t\mapsto P_{t}f\coloneq\mathscr{F}^{-1}(\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-t\lvert 2\pi\,\cdot\,\rvert^{2}}\widehat{f}(\,\cdot\,))=\mathscr{H}_{t}\ast f

where the convolution against the heat kernel
\mathscr{H}_{t}
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
is defined by

\mathscr{H}_{t}\ast f=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\mathscr{H}_{t}(\,\cdot\,-y)f(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muy=\int_{(0,1)^{2}}\mathscr{H}_{t}^{\textnormal{per}}(\,\cdot\,-y)f^{\textnormal{per}}(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muy

with
f^{\textnormal{per}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
the periodization of
f\colon\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\mathscr{H}^{\textnormal{per}}_{t}
given by

\begin{split}\mathscr{H}_{t}^{\textnormal{per}}(x)&\coloneq\frac{1}{4\pi t}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-\frac{\lvert x-n\rvert^{2}}{4t}}=\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 0.7mu\langle\omega,x\rangle}\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-t\lvert 2\pi\omega\rvert^{2}}\qquad\text{for}\penalty\ t>0,\\
\mathscr{H}_{0}^{\textnormal{per}}&\coloneq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\delta_{n},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathscr{H}_{t}^{\textnormal{per}}(x)\coloneq 0\qquad\text{for}\penalty\ t<0.\end{split}

For
f\colon[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
, we define the resolution of the heat equation as

\mathcal{I}[f]_{t}\coloneq\int_{0}^{t}P_{t-s}f_{s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus=\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{H}_{t-s}\ast f_{s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus\qquad\text{where}\penalty\ t\in[0,T].

We define the solution to the elliptic equation
-\Delta\Phi_{f}=f-\overline{f}
by

\Phi_{f}\coloneq\mathscr{G}\ast f,\qquad\text{where}\quad f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})\quad\text{and}

\mathscr{G}(x)\coloneq\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{0\}}\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 0.7mu\langle\omega,x\rangle}\frac{1}{\lvert 2\pi\omega\rvert^{2}},\qquad(\mathscr{G}\ast f)(x)=\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{0\}}\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 0.7mu\langle\omega,x\rangle}\frac{1}{\lvert 2\pi\omega\rvert^{2}}\widehat{f}(\omega).

We denote for every
\omega=(\omega^{1},\omega^{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
j=1,2
the Fourier multipliers
H^{j}_{t}(\omega)\coloneq 2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 0.7mu\omega^{j}\exp(-t\lvert 2\pi\omega\rvert^{2})\mathds{1}_{t\geq 0}
and
G^{j}(\omega)\coloneq 2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 0.7mu\omega^{j}\lvert 2\pi\omega\rvert^{-2}\mathds{1}_{\omega\neq 0}
.

Given a Banach space
E
and an interval
I\subseteq[0,\infty)
we write
C_{I}E\coloneq C(I;E)
for the space of continuous maps
f\colon I\to E
. For compact
I
we equip
C_{I}E
with the norm
\lVert f\rVert_{C_{I}E}\coloneq\sup_{t\in I}\lVert f_{t}\rVert_{E}
. For
\kappa\in(0,1)
, we define
C^{\kappa}_{I}E\coloneq C^{\kappa}(I;E)
as the completion of
C^{\infty}(I;E)
under the norm
\lVert f\rVert_{C_{I}^{\kappa}E}\coloneq\lVert f\rVert_{C_{I}E}+\sup_{s\neq t\in I}\frac{\lVert f_{t}-f_{s}\rVert_{E}}{\lvert t-s\rvert^{\kappa}}
, where
C^{\infty}(I;E)
denotes the space of smooth functions in the interior of
I
, which, along with all of their derivatives, can be continuously extended to functions in
C_{I}E
. For
T>0
, we use the shorthand
C_{T}E=C_{[0,T]}E
,
C_{T}^{\kappa}E=C_{[0,T]}^{\kappa}E
and understand
C_{T}^{0}E=C_{T}E
.
Given
\eta\geq 0
, we denote
f_{\eta\textnormal{-}\textnormal{wt}}(t)\coloneq(1\wedge t)^{\eta}f_{t}
for each
f\colon(0,T]\to E
and
t\in(0,T]
. We define the Banach space

C_{\eta;T}E\coloneq\Big\{f\colon(0,T]\to E:\penalty\ f_{\eta\textnormal{-}\textnormal{wt}}(0)\coloneq\lim_{t\to 0}f_{\eta\textnormal{-}\textnormal{wt}}(t)\in E,\penalty\ f_{\eta\textnormal{-}\textnormal{wt}}\in C_{(0,T]}E\Big\}

equipped with the norm

\lVert f\rVert_{C_{\eta;T}E}\coloneq\sup_{t\in(0,T]}(1\wedge t)^{\eta}\lVert f_{t}\rVert_{E}

and refer to
\eta
as the weight at
0
. We also make use of the notation
\mathscr{L}_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\coloneq C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha-2\kappa}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap C_{T}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
to denote an interpolation space, on which we define

\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{\mathscr{L}_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}=\max\{\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha-2\kappa}},\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{C_{T}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}\}.

We further understand
\mathscr{L}^{0}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2})=C_{T}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
. See Subsection
A.2
for properties of those spaces.

Let
T>0
,
\alpha\in(-5/2,-2)
and
\kappa\in(0,1/2)
. To carry out the rough analysis of Section
4
, we adapt the pathwise approach developed in
[
MM25
]
. These methods rely on the following non-linear space of enhanced rough noises
\mathcal{X}^{\alpha,\kappa}_{T}
, defined as the closure of the subset

\begin{split}&\Bigl\{\Theta(v,f):(v,f)\in\mathscr{L}^{\kappa}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+2}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})\times\mathscr{L}^{\kappa}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{2\alpha+5}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}),\penalty\ v_{0}=f_{0}=0\Bigr\}\\
&\qquad\subset\mathscr{L}^{\kappa}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})\times\mathscr{L}^{\kappa}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{2\alpha+4}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})\times\mathscr{L}^{\kappa}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{3\alpha+6}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathscr{L}^{\kappa}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{2\alpha+4}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}),\end{split}

where the map

\displaystyle\Theta\colon

\displaystyle(\mathscr{L}^{\kappa}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+2}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})\times\mathscr{L}^{\kappa}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{2\alpha+5}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}))

\displaystyle\to\mathscr{L}^{\kappa}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})\times\mathscr{L}^{\kappa}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{2\alpha+4}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})\times\mathscr{L}^{\kappa}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{3\alpha+6}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathscr{L}^{\kappa}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{2\alpha+4}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}),

\displaystyle(v,f)\mapsto\Theta(v,f),

is given by

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle\coloneq\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[{v}\nabla\Phi_{v}]-f,

\displaystyle\Theta(v,f)

\displaystyle\coloneq(v,Y,Y\mathbin{\varodot}\nabla\Phi_{v}+\nabla\Phi_{Y}\mathbin{\varodot}v,\nabla\mathcal{I}[v]\mathbin{\varodot}\nabla\Phi_{v}+\nabla^{2}\mathcal{I}[\Phi_{v}]\mathbin{\varodot}v)

with resonant products acting component-wise. We denote a generic element of this closure by
\mathbb{X}=(\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}},\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}},\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}},\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}})\in\mathcal{X}^{\alpha,\kappa}_{T}
and equip it with the metric induced by the norm

\lVert\mathbb{X}\rVert_{\mathcal{X}^{\alpha,\kappa}_{T}}\coloneq\max\{\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}^{\kappa}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}},\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}^{\kappa}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{2\alpha+4}},\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}^{\kappa}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{3\alpha+6}},\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}^{\kappa}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{2\alpha+4}}\}.

Given a normed vector space
(F,\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{F})

that cannot return from the cemetery state. There exists a metric
D_{T}^{F}
on
F^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
such that
D_{T}^{F}(f_{n},f)\to 0
if and only if for every
L>0
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T_{L}]}\lVert f_{n}(t)-f(t)\rVert_{F}=0,

(1.11)

where

T_{L}\coloneq T\wedge L\wedge\inf\{t\in[0,T]:\lVert f_{n}(t)\rVert_{F}>L\penalty\ \text{or}\penalty\ \lVert f(t)\rVert_{F}>L\},

cf.
[
Cha+22
, Lem. 1.2]
&
[
MM25
, Lem. 3.18]
.

Let
C^{2}_{1}((0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
be the space of functions
f\in C((0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
such that
\partial_{t}f
,
\partial_{x_{i}}f
and
\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{j}}f
exist in
C((0,T)\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
and can be extended to functions in
C((0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
for all
i,j\in\{1,2\}
.

We do not distinguish between the spaces
L^{2}([0,T]^{n}\times\mathbb{T}^{2n}\times\{1,2\}^{n};\mathbb{R})
,
L^{2}([0,T]^{n}\times\mathbb{T}^{2n};\mathbb{R}^{2n})
and
L^{2}([0,T]^{n};L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2n};\mathbb{R}^{2n}))
, which are identical up to isometric isomorphisms.

We further denote
L^{2}_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\coloneq L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}))
and
W^{1,2}_{T}\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})=W^{1,2}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
the space of functions in
L^{2}_{T}\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
with weak derivative (in time) belonging to
L^{2}_{T}\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, cf.
[
Eva98
, Subsec. 5.9.2]
. On the intersection
C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
we define the norm
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\alpha+1}}\coloneq\max\{\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}},\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\alpha+1}}\}
.

We write
\lesssim
to indicate that an inequality holds up to a constant depending on quantities that we do not keep track of or are fixed throughout. When we do wish to emphasise the dependence on certain quantities
\alpha
,
p
,
d
, we either write
\lesssim_{\alpha,p,d}
or define
C\coloneq C(\alpha,p,d)>0
and write
\leq C
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space that is large enough to support two independent space-time white noises
\xi^{1},\xi^{2}
on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
as specified in Subsection
B.1
. Given
\bm{\xi}\coloneq(\xi^{1},\xi^{2})
, we can define a family of complex Brownian motions by
W^{j}(t,\omega)\coloneq\bm{\xi}(\mathds{1}_{[0,t]}(\,\cdot\,)\otimes\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 0.7mu\langle\omega,\,\cdot\,\rangle}\otimes\delta_{j,\,\cdot\,})
where
t\geq 0
,
\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
j=1,2
, see Lemma
B.4
. This probability space will be fixed, so that whenever a property holds almost surely, it will do so with respect to
\mathbb{P}
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
T>0
and
[0,T]^{n}_{>}\coloneq\{(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})\in[0,T]^{n}:u_{1}>u_{2}>\ldots>u_{n}\}
. We define the iterated Itô integral, acting on
\phi\in L^{2}([0,T]^{n}_{>}\times\mathbb{T}^{2n};\mathbb{R}^{2n})
, by

I^{n}(\phi)\coloneq\sum_{\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}=1}^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muW^{j_{1}}(u_{1},\omega_{1})\ldots\int_{0}^{u_{n-1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muW^{j_{n}}(u_{n},\omega_{n})\widehat{\phi}(u_{1},-\omega_{1},j_{1},\ldots,u_{n},-\omega_{n},j_{n}),

(1.12)

where

\widehat{\phi}(u_{1},\omega_{1},j_{1},\ldots,u_{n},\omega_{n},j_{n})\coloneq\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{2})^{n}}\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 0.7mu(\langle\omega_{1},x_{1}\rangle+\ldots+\langle\omega_{n},x_{n}\rangle)}h(u_{1},x_{1},j_{1},\ldots,u_{n},x_{n},j_{n})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mux_{1}\ldots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mux_{n}.

For
n=0
, it is conventional to set
I^{0}\coloneq\textnormal{id}_{\mathbb{R}}
.

We define the cut-off

\begin{split}&\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\penalty\ \text{to be of compact support},\\
&\textnormal{supp}(\varphi)\subset B(0,1),\penalty\ \text{even}\penalty\ \text{and such that}\penalty\ \varphi(0)=1.\end{split}

(1.13)

Given
\varphi
satisfying (
1.13
), we define a sequence of mollifiers
(\psi_{\delta})_{\delta>0}
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
by

\psi_{\delta}(x)\coloneq\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 0.7mu\langle\omega,x\rangle}\varphi(\delta\omega).

(1.14)

2
Analysis of the Deterministic Keller–Segel Equation

In this section we show the existence, uniqueness and regularity of weak solutions
f
to the deterministic Keller–Segel equation,

\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} (\partial_{t}-\Delta)f&=-\chi\nabla\cdot(f\nabla\Phi_{f}),&&\penalty\ \text{in}\penalty\ (0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2},\\
-\Delta\Phi_{f}&=f-\overline{f},&&\penalty\ \text{in}\penalty\ (0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2},\\
f|_{t=0}&=f_{0},&&\penalty\ \text{on}\penalty\ \mathbb{T}^{2},\end{aligned}\end{cases}

(2.1)

where we denote the mean of each
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
by
\overline{u}=\langle u,1\rangle_{L^{2}}
. We further establish the regularity of
\sqrt{f}
in a suitable Bessel potential space (Theorem
2.2
& Lemma
2.6
).

Let us first define what we understand to be a weak solution to (
2.1
).

Definition 2.1
.

Let
f_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
,
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
and
T>0
. Then,
f\in C_{T}L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
is a weak solution to (
2.1
) on
[0,T]
if for all
t\in[0,T]
and
\psi\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
,

\langle f_{t},\psi\rangle_{L^{2}}=\langle f_{0},\psi\rangle_{L^{2}}+\chi\int_{0}^{t}\langle f_{s}\nabla\Phi_{f_{s}},\nabla\psi\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus.

2.1
Existence and Regularity

We assume that the initial data
f_{0}
lies in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, is non-negative and of unit mass. We refer to
[
Per04
,
HP09
,
Hor03
,
Hor04
]
and Remark
2.3
for a wider ranging survey of the derivation and properties of the Cauchy problem (
2.1
) and related PDE.

Our main emphasis is to recall that the global well-posedness of (
2.1
) depends on the sign and size of
\chi
. For small
\chi
we can employ the periodic Gagliardo–Nirenberg–Sobolev inequality (
A.3
) to show that the solution will be globally well-posed (cf. Theorem
2.2
), whereas for large
\chi
it is known that the solution may blow up in finite time
[
KX16
, Thm. 8.1]
.

In stating the main result of this subsection (Theorem
2.2
) we use the notation
C_{\textnormal{gns-per}}(2,3,1)
to denote the optimal constant such that the periodic Gagliardo–Nirenberg–Sobolev inequality (
A.3
) holds, which is properly elucidated in Subsection
A.1
below.

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
f_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
f_{0}\geq 0
and
\overline{f_{0}}=1
, and
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
. Then the following hold:

1.

There exists a
T^{*}=T^{*}(\chi,\lVert f_{0}\rVert_{L^{2}})\in(0,\infty]
and a unique map
f\colon[0,T^{*})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
such that
f\in C_{T}L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L^{2}_{T}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
for every
T\in(0,T^{*})
,
f|_{t=0}=f_{0}
and
f
solves (
2.1
) in the weak sense on
[0,T]
(see Definition
2.1
). Furthermore for each
t\in[0,T^{*})
this solution is non-negative almost everywhere and
\lVert f_{t}\rVert_{L^{1}}=1
. Finally
T^{*}
is such that either
T^{*}=\infty
or
\lim_{t\nearrow T^{*}}\lVert f_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}=\infty
, and we refer to it as the maximal time of existence (for
f
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
.)

2.

If
\chi\leq\chi^{*}\coloneq C_{\textnormal{gns-per}}(2,3,1)^{-3}
, then one has
T^{*}=\infty
.

3.

It holds that
f\in C^{2}_{1}((0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
for every
T\in(0,T^{*})
. Further if
f_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
for some
\gamma>0
, then
f\in C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
.

Proof.

Since these statements are mostly amalgams of known results we only provide a sketched proof with references for full details.

The proof of Claim
1
is carried out through a Galerkin approximation and is classical. In the following we give a brief sketch: Testing the equation against itself, we obtain the a priori energy identity

\lVert f_{t}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}=\lVert f_{0}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}-2\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla f_{s}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus+\chi\int_{0}^{t}\lVert f_{s}\rVert^{3}_{L^{3}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus-\chi\int_{0}^{t}\lVert f_{s}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus.

(2.2)

We identify

\lVert f_{s}\rVert_{L^{3}}^{3}-\lVert f_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}=\langle f_{s}-\overline{f_{s}},f_{s}^{2}\rangle_{L^{2}}

and bound for some
C>0
by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, the Sobolev embedding
\mathcal{H}^{1/2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\hookrightarrow L^{4}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
(cf. (
A.1
)) and real interpolation,

\lvert\langle f_{s}-\overline{f_{s}},f_{s}^{2}\rangle_{L^{2}}\rvert\leq\lVert f_{s}-\overline{f_{s}}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert f_{s}\rVert_{L^{4}}^{2}\leq\lVert f_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert f_{s}\rVert_{L^{4}}^{2}\leq C\lVert f_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert f_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1/2}}^{2}\leq C\lVert f_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\lVert f_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}.

Therefore, it follows by (
2.2
) and an application of Young’s inequality that for each
\vartheta>0
,

\lVert f_{t}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq\lVert f_{0}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}-2\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla f_{s}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus+\frac{C^{2}\chi^{2}}{2\vartheta}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert f_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{4}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus+\frac{\vartheta}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert f_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus.

Using the identity
\lVert f_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}^{2}=\lVert f_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert\nabla f_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
, we obtain

\lVert f_{t}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq\lVert f_{0}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}-\Bigl(2-\frac{\vartheta}{2}\Bigr)\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla f_{s}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus+\frac{C^{2}\chi^{2}}{2\vartheta}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert f_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{4}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus+\frac{\vartheta}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert f_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus.

We can then choose
\vartheta=2
to deduce

\lVert f_{t}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq\lVert f_{0}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}-\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla f_{s}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus+\frac{C^{2}\chi^{2}}{4}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert f_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{4}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus+\int_{0}^{t}\lVert f_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus.

A comparison argument to the ODE
\dot{x}(t)=C^{2}\chi^{2}x(t)^{2}/4+x(t)
shows that there exists a time horizon
T^{*}
such that
t\mapsto\lVert f_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
and
t\mapsto\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla f_{s}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus
are locally bounded on
[0,T^{*})
.

To prove uniqueness, let
g
be another weak solution with
g_{0}=f_{0}
. By a similar calculation, one finds a priori that

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mu}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mut}\lVert f_{t}-g_{t}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}+\lVert\nabla(f_{t}-g_{t})\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}&=\chi\Bigl\langle f_{t}\nabla\Phi_{f_{t}-g_{t}}+(f_{t}-g_{t})\nabla\Phi_{g_{t}},\nabla(f_{t}-g_{t})\Bigr\rangle_{L^{2}}\\
&\leq\lvert\chi\rvert\Bigl(\lVert f_{t}\nabla\Phi_{f_{t}-g_{t}}\rVert_{L^{2}}+\lVert(f_{t}-g_{t})\nabla\Phi_{g_{t}}\rVert_{L^{2}}\Bigr)\lVert\nabla(f_{t}-g_{t})\rVert_{L^{2}}\\
&\lesssim\lvert\chi\rvert\Bigl(\lVert f_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\lVert\nabla\Phi_{f_{t}-g_{t}}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\lVert f_{t}-g_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert\nabla\Phi_{g_{t}}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{2}}\Bigr)\lVert\nabla(f_{t}-g_{t})\rVert_{L^{2}}\\
&\lesssim\lvert\chi\rvert\Bigl(\lVert f_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\lVert g_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\Bigr)\lVert f_{t}-g_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert\nabla(f_{t}-g_{t})\rVert_{L^{2}},\end{split}

where in the second inequality we used that
uv\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
for each
(u,v)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\times\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
or
(u,v)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\times\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, see
[
BH21
, Thm. 7.4]
. A combination of Young’s and Gronwall’s inequalities then implies that there exists some
C>0
such that

\lVert f_{t}-g_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\lVert f_{0}-g_{0}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\exp\Bigl(C\chi^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert f_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}^{2}+\lVert g_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus\Bigr),

from which uniqueness follows on the interval
[0,T^{*})
.

Taking the constant function
1
as a test function shows that the mean of the solution is preserved. The almost everywhere non-negativity can be shown by testing with (a smooth approximant to) the negative part of the solution
f_{t}^{-}\coloneq\min\{0,f_{t}\}
and taking limits by the dominated convergence theorem. For a similar proof in the two-dimensional plane, see
[
MT23
, Prop. 3.4]
.
A stronger notion of positivity of solutions given initial data that are continuous and bounded away from
0
is shown in Lemma
2.5
.

To prove Claim
2
we use that for
T<T^{*}
and weak solutions
f\in C_{T}L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L^{2}_{T}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
one has for each
t\leq T
the identity (
2.2
).

We first note that if
\chi\leq 0
then by Gronwall’s inequality applied to (
2.2
) we have a direct bound for
\lVert f_{t}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}
which is sufficient to show that
\lVert f_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}<\infty
for all
t<\infty
.

In the case
\chi>0
, we need an energy identity for the re-centred solution
f_{t}-\overline{f_{t}}
. Noting that
\overline{f_{t}}\equiv 1
, we obtain

\lVert f_{t}-\overline{f_{t}}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}=\lVert f_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}-1

and

\lVert f_{t}\rVert_{L^{3}}^{3}-\lVert f_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}=\lVert f_{t}-\overline{f_{t}}\rVert_{L^{3}}^{3}+2\lVert f_{t}-\overline{f_{t}}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Hence, we can deduce from (
2.2
),

\lVert f_{t}-\overline{f_{t}}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}+1=\lVert f_{0}-\overline{f_{0}}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}+1-2\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla f_{s}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus+\chi\int_{0}^{t}\lVert f_{s}-\overline{f_{s}}\rVert^{3}_{L^{3}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus+2\chi\int_{0}^{t}\lVert f_{s}-\overline{f_{s}}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus,

which yields

\lVert f_{t}-\overline{f_{t}}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}=\lVert f_{0}-\overline{f_{0}}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}-2\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla f_{s}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus+\chi\int_{0}^{t}\lVert f_{s}-\overline{f_{s}}\rVert^{3}_{L^{3}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus+2\chi\int_{0}^{t}\lVert f_{s}-\overline{f_{s}}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus.

(2.3)

An application of the periodic Gagliardo–Nirenberg–Sobolev inequality (
A.3
) with
d=2
,
q=3
and
r=1
yields

\lVert f_{s}-\overline{f_{s}}\rVert^{3}_{L^{3}}\leq C_{\textnormal{gns-per}}(2,3,1)^{3}\lVert\nabla f_{s}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\lVert f_{s}-\overline{f_{s}}\rVert_{L^{1}}\leq 2C_{\textnormal{gns-per}}(2,3,1)^{3}\lVert\nabla f_{s}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}},

where in the second step we used Minkowski’s inequality to estimate
\lVert f_{s}-\overline{f_{s}}\rVert_{L^{1}}\leq 2
.
Inserting this into (
2.3
) we find the inequality

\lVert f_{t}-\overline{f_{t}}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq\lVert f_{0}-\overline{f_{0}}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}-2\Bigl(1-\chi C_{\textnormal{gns-per}}(2,3,1)^{3}\Bigr)\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\nabla f_{s}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus+2\chi\int_{0}^{t}\lVert f_{s}-\overline{f_{s}}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus.

If
\chi\leq\chi^{*}\coloneq C_{\textnormal{gns-per}}(2,3,1)^{-3}
then

\lVert f_{t}-\overline{f_{t}}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq\lVert f_{0}-\overline{f_{0}}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}+2\chi\int_{0}^{t}\lVert f_{s}-\overline{f_{s}}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus

and we can apply Gronwall’s inequality to deduce

\lVert f_{t}-\overline{f_{t}}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq\lVert f_{0}-\overline{f_{0}}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}}\exp(2\chi t),

which implies
T^{*}=\infty
.

To prove Claim
3
we first use the fact that weak solutions to (
2.1
) are mild,

f=Pf_{0}-\chi\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[f\nabla\Phi_{f}],

(2.4)

which can be shown by testing the equation against the time-reversed heat kernel.
Using (
2.4
) we can bootstrap the space regularity of
f
at positive times to establish
f\in C((0,T];\mathcal{H}^{4}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
, which by Sobolev’s embedding
\mathcal{H}^{4}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\hookrightarrow C^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
is enough to deduce
\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{j}}f\in C((0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
for every
i,j\in\{1,2\}
. Furthermore, we can use the same space regularity to deduce that the weak time derivative
\partial_{t}f
lies in
C((0,T];\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
, which together with the embedding
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\hookrightarrow C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
(cf. (
A.2
)) yields
\partial_{t}f\in C((0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
. The fundamental theorem of calculus then allows us to argue that the weak time derivative coincides with the (usual) time derivative, which yields
f\in C^{2}_{1}((0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
.

To establish
f\in C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
for every
\gamma>0
,
f_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
and
T<T^{*}
, it suffices to estimate each term in the mild formulation (
2.4
) by using Schauder’s estimates (Lemma
A.3
) combined with product estimates in Bessel potential spaces.
This yields Claim
3
.
∎

Remark 2.3

(Sharp a priori bounds)
.

On the whole space instead of the torus, one can make use of the sharp Gagliardo–Nirenberg–Sobolev inequality found in
[
Wei83
]
to show that the entropy is strictly non-increasing and the solution is globally well-posed provided
\chi<4\pi(1.86225\ldots)
, see
[
BDP06
, Sec. 2.2]
. Still on the whole space, this range can be further improved to allow for all
\chi<8\pi
by instead analysing the evolution of the free energy and replacing the Gagliardo–Nirenberg–Sobolev inequality with the logarithmic Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev inequality, see
[
BDP06
]
. This result is in fact sharp. A straightforward argument, see
[
BDP06
, Sec. 2.1]
, analysing the evolution of the second moment of solutions demonstrates that weak and very weak solutions to (
2.1
) must blow up in finite time provided
\chi>8\pi
with a blow-up time converging to
+\infty
as
\chi
approaches
8\pi
. See also more recent results that treat more general initial data
[
Wei18
,
FT23
]
. The picture on the torus is more complicated
[
KX16
, Thm. 8.1]
; however, for our purposes we do not require a sharp result, only well-posedness of the PDE (
2.1
) for some range of
\chi
.

Next, we show that the solution to (
2.1
) started from continuous initial data is continuous everywhere.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
f_{0}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
f_{0}\geq 0
and
\overline{f_{0}}=1
,
f
be the weak solution to (
2.1
) with initial data
f_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence (see Theorem
2.2
). Then
f\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
for every
T<T^{*}
and
T^{*}
coincides with the maximal time of existence in
C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
.

Proof.

Using that
f_{0}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{2})\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, we can apply Theorem
2.2
to find the unique weak solution
f\in C((0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
; hence it suffices to establish the continuity of
f
at time
0
, i.e. on a small time interval
[0,\bar{T}]
where
\bar{T}\in(0,T\wedge 1]
. By the proof of Theorem
2.2
(cf. (
2.4
)), it follows that the weak solution is also mild, hence we can use Young’s convolution estimate and Schauder’s lemma to bound for every
\vartheta\in(0,1)
,

\lVert f_{t}\rVert_{C(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\leq\lVert f_{0}\rVert_{C(\mathbb{T}^{2})}+C\lvert\chi\rvert\lVert f\rVert_{C_{\bar{T}}L^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\lvert t-s\rvert^{-\frac{1+\vartheta}{2}}\lVert f_{s}\rVert_{C(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus,

where
C
is some constant. Denote
\beta\coloneq\frac{2}{1-\vartheta}
, we can now apply the fractional Gronwall estimate
[
Web19
, Rem. 3.7]
to deduce

\lVert f_{t}\rVert_{C_{\bar{T}}C(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\leq\beta\lVert f_{0}\rVert_{C(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\exp\Bigl(C_{\beta}\lvert\chi\rvert^{\beta}\lVert f\rVert_{C_{\bar{T}}L^{2}}^{\beta}t\Bigr),

where
C_{\beta}>0
is a constant that only depends on
\beta
. This a priori estimate allows us to use an approximation argument to show that
f
is continuous at time
0
which implies
f\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
.
∎

2.2
Boundedness Away From Zero

In this subsection we show that the solution to (
2.1
) constructed in Theorem
2.2
is bounded away from zero uniformly in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
for every
T<T^{*}
, if its initial data is assumed to be continuous and positive.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
f_{0}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
f_{0}>0
and
\overline{f_{0}}=1
,
f
be the weak solution to (
2.1
) with initial data
f_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence (see Lemma
2.4
). Then for all
T<T^{*}
there exists a
c>0
such that
f(t,x)\geq c
for all
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
.

Proof.

It follows by Theorem
2.2
and Lemma
2.4
that
f\in C^{2}_{1}((0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
is a classical solution to (
2.1
). Denote
b\coloneq-\chi\nabla\Phi_{f}
, it then follows by elliptic regularity
[
MM25
, Lem. A.8]
that
b\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\nabla\cdot b=\chi(f-\overline{f})\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})
. Hence we can apply Proposition
A.4
to deduce that there exist some
c>0
such that
f(t,x)\geq c
for all
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
.
∎

2.3
Regularity of the Square Root

In this subsection we establish the regularity of the square root of the solution to the deterministic Keller–Segel equation (
2.1
) (Lemma
2.6
).

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
f_{0}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
f_{0}\geq 0
and
\overline{f_{0}}=1
,
f
be the weak solution to (
2.1
) with initial data
f_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence (see Lemma
2.4
). Then for all
T<T^{*}
it holds that
\sqrt{f}\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
. Assume further
f_{0}>0
and
f_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
for some
\gamma\geq 0
, then it holds that
\sqrt{f}\in C_{\eta;T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+2\eta}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
for every
\eta\in[0,1/2)
.

Proof.

Given
f_{0}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
such that
f_{0}\geq 0
and
\overline{f_{0}}=1
, we can apply Lemma
2.4
to construct
f\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
for all
T<T^{*}
. Using the continuity of the square root in the non-negative reals, it follows that
\sqrt{f}\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
.

If further
f_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
for some
\gamma\geq 0
, we can apply Theorem
2.2
and Lemma
2.4
to construct
f\in C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
. Recall from (
2.4
) the mild formulation

f=Pf_{0}-\chi\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[f\nabla\Phi_{f}].

We can bound the contribution from the initial data by Schauder’s estimate
[
MM25
, Lem. A.5]
,

\lVert Pf_{0}\rVert_{C_{\eta;T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+2\eta}}\lesssim\lVert f_{0}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}}

and the contribution from the nonlinearity by Lemma
A.3
combined with a product estimate,

\lVert\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[f\nabla\Phi_{f}]\rVert_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+2\eta}}\lesssim\lVert\mathcal{I}[f\nabla\Phi_{f}]\rVert_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1+2\eta}}\lesssim_{T}\lVert f\nabla\Phi_{f}\rVert_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}}^{2},\quad\text{if}\penalty\ \gamma>0

and

\lVert\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[f\nabla\Phi_{f}]\rVert_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{2\eta}}\lesssim\lVert\mathcal{I}[f\nabla\Phi_{f}]\rVert_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{1+2\eta}}\lesssim_{T}\lVert f\nabla\Phi_{f}\rVert_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{-(1-2\eta)/2}}\lesssim\lVert f\rVert_{C_{T}L^{2}}^{2},\quad\text{if}\penalty\ \gamma=0.

Hence, we obtain
f\in C_{\eta;T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+2\eta}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
for all
T<T^{*}
.
If in addition
f_{0}>0
, it also follows by Lemma
2.5
that there exists a constant
c>0
such that
f\geq c
on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
. To prove
\sqrt{f}\in C_{\eta;T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+2\eta}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, it then suffices to apply
[
BCD11
, Cor. 2.91]
combined with a smooth cut-off. To prove
\sqrt{f}\in L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, we apply Lemma
A.5
with
F=\sqrt{\,\cdot\,}
to deduce the existence of a constant
C\coloneq C(\gamma+1,F,\lVert f\rVert_{C_{T}L^{\infty}},c^{-1})>0
such that

\bigl\lVert\sqrt{f}\bigr\rVert_{L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}}^{2}=\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\lVert\sqrt{f_{t}}\bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mut\leq\int_{0}^{T}C^{2}\lVert f_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mut\leq C^{2}\lVert f\rVert_{L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}}^{2},

which yields the claim.
∎

3
Regular Theory for Small Noise Intensities

In this section we consider solutions
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
to the additive-noise approximation (
1.8
). As discussed in Subsection
1.2
, the choice of state space affects the relative scaling between
\varepsilon
and
\delta(\varepsilon)
, which becomes more restrictive as we consider increasingly regular spaces. Here we consider the regular setting, i.e. the Bessel potential space
\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
of regularity
\gamma\in[-1,0]
and the relative scaling
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta(\varepsilon)^{-\gamma-2}\to 0
as
\varepsilon,\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
; for the rough setting see Section
4
.

We can deduce increasingly stronger results by decreasing the range of
\gamma
. The most general range is
\gamma\in[-1,0]
, for which we establish the well-posedness of the stochastic heat equation
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}
(Proposition
E.4
) and a law of large numbers for
(\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
(Lemma
3.2
). The lower bound
\gamma\geq-1
is used to make sense of the product
\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\bm{\xi}^{\delta}
that appears in
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}
, see the proof of Proposition
E.4
for details.
The smaller range
\gamma\in(-1/2,0]
allows us to establish the well-posedness of
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
in
\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
(Lemma
3.1
) and a law of large numbers (Theorem
3.3
).
To establish large deviation principles, we require some compactness, which we achieve by assuming
\gamma<1
. As such, we obtain a large deviation principle for
(\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
in
\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
in the range of regularities
\gamma\in[-1,0)
(Theorem
3.4
) and one for
(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
in the range
\gamma\in(-1/2,0)
(Theorem
3.5
).

Throughout this section we consider initial data
\rho_{0}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\overline{\rho_{0}}=1
. In particular, this ensures that
\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
(see Lemma
2.6
) and that
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}\in C_{T}L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
(see Proposition
E.4
).

3.1
Well-Posedness

In this subsection we construct solutions to (
1.8
) with initial data in
C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
taking values in the Bessel potential space
\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
of regularity
\gamma\in(-1/2,0]
(Lemma
3.1
).

Recall that
(\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
denotes the space of continuous functions that may blow up in finite time (see Subsection
1.3
).

Lemma 3.1
.

\rho_{0}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
