-.95)(+.22,-.95)(+.17,-.75)\strokepath\moveto(0,-.5)\curveto(-.1,-.5)(-.2,-.5)(-.15,-.4)\curveto(-.1,-.1)(.1,-.1)(.15,-.4)\curveto(.2,-.5)(.1,-.5)(0,-.5)\fillpath\moveto(-.15,0)\curveto(-.15,.1)(-.15,.21)(-.4,.2)\curveto(-.6,.19)(-.69,.1)(-.7,0)\curveto(-.71,-.1)(-.6,-.3)(-.5,-.3)\curveto(-.4,-.3)(-.15,-.2)(-.15,0)\fillpath\cbezier(0,1.333336)(-1,1.333336)(-1.2,0.5)(-1,0)\cbezier(-1,0)(-1.05,-.9)(-.5,-.3)(-.5,-.65)\moveto(+.15,0)\curveto(+.15,.1)(+.15,.21)(+.4,.2)\curveto(+.6,.19)(+.69,.1)(+.7,0)\curveto(+.71,-.1)(+.6,-.3)(+.5,-.3)\curveto(+.4,-.3)(+.15,-.2)(+.15,0)\fillpath\cbezier(0,1.333336)(+1,1.333336)(+1.2,0.5)(+1,0)\cbezier(+1,0)(+1.05,-.9)(+.5,-.3)(+.5,-.65)\endpicture}{\hbox{}\picture(2.48,0.0)(-1.24,-1.0)\roundcap\moveto(-.33,-.7)\curveto(-.3,-.6)(-.22,-.6)(-.19,-.75)\curveto(-.15,-.9)(-.38,-.9)(-.33,-.7)\strokepath\moveto(-.5,-.7)\curveto(-.45,-.6)(-.4,-.6)(-.35,-.7)\curveto(-.4,-1.2)(-.5,-1.2)(-.5,-.7)\strokepath\moveto(-.17,-.75)\curveto(-.13,-.65)(-.05,-.62)(-.02,-.75)\curveto(+.03,-.95)(-.22,-.95)(-.17,-.75)\strokepath\moveto(+.33,-.7)\curveto(+.3,-.6)(+.22,-.6)(+.19,-.75)\curveto(+.15,-.9)(+.38,-.9)(+.33,-.7)\strokepath\moveto(+.5,-.7)\curveto(+.45,-.6)(+.4,-.6)(+.35,-.7)\curveto(+.4,-1.2)(+.5,-1.2)(+.5,-.7)\strokepath\moveto(+.17,-.75)\curveto(+.13,-.65)(+.05,-.62)(+.02,-.75)\curveto(-.03,-.95)(+.22,-.95)(+.17,-.75)\strokepath\moveto(0,-.5)\curveto(-.1,-.5)(-.2,-.5)(-.15,-.4)\curveto(-.1,-.1)(.1,-.1)(.15,-.4)\curveto(.2,-.5)(.1,-.5)(0,-.5)\fillpath\moveto(-.15,0)\curveto(-.15,.1)(-.15,.21)(-.4,.2)\curveto(-.6,.19)(-.69,.1)(-.7,0)\curveto(-.71,-.1)(-.6,-.3)(-.5,-.3)\curveto(-.4,-.3)(-.15,-.2)(-.15,0)\fillpath\cbezier(0,1.333336)(-1,1.333336)(-1.2,0.5)(-1,0)\cbezier(-1,0)(-1.05,-.9)(-.5,-.3)(-.5,-.65)\moveto(+.15,0)\curveto(+.15,.1)(+.15,.21)(+.4,.2)\curveto(+.6,.19)(+.69,.1)(+.7,0)\curveto(+.71,-.1)(+.6,-.3)(+.5,-.3)\curveto(+.4,-.3)(+.15,-.2)(+.15,0)\fillpath\cbezier(0,1.333336)(+1,1.333336)(+1.2,0.5)(+1,0)\cbezier(+1,0)(+1.05,-.9)(+.5,-.3)(+.5,-.65)\endpicture}{\hbox{}\picture(2.48,0.0)(-1.24,-1.0)\roundcap\moveto(-.33,-.7)\curveto(-.3,-.6)(-.22,-.6)(-.19,-.75)\curveto(-.15,-.9)(-.38,-.9)(-.33,-.7)\strokepath\moveto(-.5,-.7)\curveto(-.45,-.6)(-.4,-.6)(-.35,-.7)\curveto(-.4,-1.2)(-.5,-1.2)(-.5,-.7)\strokepath\moveto(-.17,-.75)\curveto(-.13,-.65)(-.05,-.62)(-.02,-.75)\curveto(+.03,-.95)(-.22,-.95)(-.17,-.75)\strokepath\moveto(+.33,-.7)\curveto(+.3,-.6)(+.22,-.6)(+.19,-.75)\curveto(+.15,-.9)(+.38,-.9)(+.33,-.7)\strokepath\moveto(+.5,-.7)\curveto(+.45,-.6)(+.4,-.6)(+.35,-.7)\curveto(+.4,-1.2)(+.5,-1.2)(+.5,-.7)\strokepath\moveto(+.17,-.75)\curveto(+.13,-.65)(+.05,-.62)(+.02,-.75)\curveto(-.03,-.95)(+.22,-.95)(+.17,-.75)\strokepath\moveto(0,-.5)\curveto(-.1,-.5)(-.2,-.5)(-.15,-.4)\curveto(-.1,-.1)(.1,-.1)(.15,-.4)\curveto(.2,-.5)(.1,-.5)(0,-.5)\fillpath\moveto(-.15,0)\curveto(-.15,.1)(-.15,.21)(-.4,.2)\curveto(-.6,.19)(-.69,.1)(-.7,0)\curveto(-.71,-.1)(-.6,-.3)(-.5,-.3)\curveto(-.4,-.3)(-.15,-.2)(-.15,0)\fillpath\cbezier(0,1.333336)(-1,1.333336)(-1.2,0.5)(-1,0)\cbezier(-1,0)(-1.05,-.9)(-.5,-.3)(-.5,-.65)\moveto(+.15,0)\curveto(+.15,.1)(+.15,.21)(+.4,.2)\curveto(+.6,.19)(+.69,.1)(+.7,0)\curveto(+.71,-.1)(+.6,-.3)(+.5,-.3)\curveto(+.4,-.3)(+.15,-.2)(+.15,0)\fillpath\cbezier(0,1.333336)(+1,1.333336)(+1.2,0.5)(+1,0)\cbezier(+1,0)(+1.05,-.9)(+.5,-.3)(+.5,-.65)\endpicture}}}}_{\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}}[\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta}]\in(0,T]
denotes the maximal time of existence of
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta}
.) In particular, the solution map is almost surely locally Lipschitz continuous in the noise
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\in C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
.

Proof.

An application of Proposition
E.4
yields
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}\in C_{T}L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
almost surely for each
\delta>0
. Given a realisation of the noise,
\varepsilon>0
,
\gamma\in(-1/2,0]
and
\bar{T}
sufficiently small, we can construct a local solution
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta}
to (
1.8
) as a fixed point in
C_{\bar{T}}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{\bar{T}}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
. Re-starting the equation, we may then iterate the construction to obtain a solution in
(\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
. Since the noise is additive in (
1.8
), it follows that the solution map is continuous in
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\in C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
.
∎

3.2
Law of Large Numbers

In this subsection we establish a (weak) law of large numbers for solutions
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
to (
1.8
) in
(\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
under the relative scaling
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta(\varepsilon)^{-\gamma-2}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
for some
\gamma\in(-1/2,0]
(Theorem
3.3
). It is convenient to first prove a law of large numbers for
(\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
(Lemma
3.2
), which we can then transfer to
(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
by the continuous mapping theorem (Theorem
3.3
).

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\overline{\rho_{0}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
2.1
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence (see Lemma
2.4
). Assume
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta(\varepsilon)^{-\gamma-2}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
for some
\gamma\in[-1,0]
. Then for all
T<T^{*}
, it follows that
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\to 0
in
C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
in probability as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Proof.

An application of Proposition
E.4
combined with the relative scaling
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta(\varepsilon)^{\gamma-2}\to 0
yields

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}\rVert_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}}^{2}\Bigr]^{1/2}\lesssim\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\rVert_{C_{T}L^{2}\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(1+\delta(\varepsilon)^{-\gamma-2})=0,

which implies convergence in probability by Markov’s inequality.
∎

The following theorem demonstrates an analogue of the law of large numbers for
(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
in
(\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
. Note that in contrast to Lemma
3.2
we have to restrict the range of
\gamma
to
\gamma\in(-1/2,0]
so that we have enough regularity to make sense of
the non-linearity in (
1.8
) (see also Lemma
3.1
).

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\overline{\rho_{0}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
2.1
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence (see Lemma
2.4
). Assume
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta(\varepsilon)^{-\gamma-2}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
for some
\gamma\in(-1/2,0]
. Then for all
T<T^{*}
, it follows that
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}\to\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
in
(\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
in probability as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Proof.

It follows by Lemma
3.2
and the relative scaling
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta(\varepsilon)^{-\gamma-2}\to 0
that
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\to 0
in
C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
in probability as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Using the local Lipschitz continuity of
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
in
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}
(Lemma
3.1
), it follows by the continuous mapping theorem that
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}\to\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
in
(\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
in probability as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
∎

3.3
Large Deviation Principle

In this subsection we we apply the weak-convergence approach to large deviations
[
BDM08
]
to establish a large deviation principle for solutions
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
to (
1.8
) in
(\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
under the relative scaling
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta(\varepsilon)^{-\gamma-2}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
for some
\gamma\in(-1/2,0)
(Theorem
3.5
). As in Subsection
3.2
, we first consider the noise
(\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
driving
(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
(cf. Lemma
3.1
) for which we establish a large deviation principle (Theorem
3.4
) that we can then transfer to
(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
via the contraction principle (Theorem
3.5
).

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\overline{\rho_{0}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
2.1
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence (see Lemma
2.4
). Assume
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta(\varepsilon)^{-\gamma-2}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
for some
\gamma\in[-1,0)
. Then for all
T<T^{*}
, it follows that the sequence
(\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
satisfies a large deviation principle in
C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
with speed
\varepsilon
and good rate function

\mathscr{I}_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}}(s)\coloneq\inf\Bigl\{\frac{1}{2}\lVert h\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})}:\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}=s\Bigr\},\qquad\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}\coloneq\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}h].

For the existence and regularity of
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}
, see also Lemmas
D.1
&
D.2
.

Proof.

It follows from (
E.2
) that
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}
is a functional of finitely many Brownian motions with the number of Brownian drivers increasing to
\infty
as
\delta\to 0
. Hence to deduce a large deviation principle we apply
[
BDM08
, Thm. 6]
, which is based on an infinite-dimensional version of the Boué–Dupuis formula.

First let us introduce some notation. We define for every
M<\infty
the closed ball

S^{M}\coloneq\{h\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2}):\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq M\}

equipped with the weak topology, which is metrizable (resp. compact) by the separability (resp. reflexivity) of
L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
(see
[
Bre11
, Thm. 3.29 & Thm. 3.18]
). We further denote

L^{2}_{\textnormal{pred}}\coloneq\Bigl\{\phi\colon\Omega\times[0,T]\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\penalty\ \text{predictable}:\lVert\phi\rVert_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})}<\infty\penalty\ \text{a.s.}\Bigr\}

and

L^{2,M}_{\textnormal{pred}}\coloneq\{\phi\in L^{2}_{\textnormal{pred}}:\phi\in S^{M}\penalty\ \text{a.s.}\}.

For every sequence
(u^{(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
such that
u^{(\varepsilon)}\in L^{2,M}_{\textnormal{pred}}
for each
\varepsilon>0
and
u^{(\varepsilon)}\rightharpoonup u
in
S^{M}
in law as
\varepsilon\to 0
(i.e. as
S^{M}
-valued random variables), define
u^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta}\coloneq u^{(\varepsilon)}\ast\psi_{\delta}
, where
\psi_{\delta}
is as in (
1.14
).

To apply
[
BDM08
, Thm. 6]
in our context, we need to show that for every
M>0
,
(u^{(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
and
\gamma\in[-1,0)
:

1.

The set
\Gamma_{M}\coloneq\{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}:h\in S^{M}\}\subset C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
is compact in the strong topology induced by the norm
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}}
.

2.

It holds that
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}+\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{u^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}}\to\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{u}
in
C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
in law as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Let us first show
1
. For the existence and regularity of
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}=\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}h]
, see Appendix
D
. The weak derivative of
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}
is given by
\partial_{t}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}\coloneq\Delta\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}+\nabla\cdot(\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}h)
, hence Lemma
D.2
implies for every
h\in S^{M}
the bound

\max\{\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}\rVert_{L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}},\lVert\partial_{t}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}\rVert_{L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{-1}}\}\lesssim\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\rVert_{C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\rVert_{C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}M.

(3.1)

For every
R>0
define the set

A_{R}\coloneq\Bigl\{u\in L^{2}_{T}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap W^{1,2}_{T}\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}):\lVert u\rVert_{L^{2}_{T}\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\lVert\partial_{t}u\rVert_{L^{2}_{T}\mathcal{H}^{-1}}\leq R\Bigr\},

it then follows by (
3.1
) that
\Gamma_{M}\subset A_{R}
for
R
sufficiently large depending on
\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\rVert_{C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}M
. An application of
[
Eva98
, Sec. 5.9, Thm. 3]
combined with the Aubin–Lions lemma yields that the set
A_{R}
is totally bounded in
C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, where we used the compact embeddings
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
and
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
(see Lemma
A.9
). Using that every subset of a totally bounded set is again totally bounded, it follows that
\Gamma_{M}
is itself totally bounded in
C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
. Hence to show that
\Gamma_{M}
is compact it suffices to show that it is closed.

Let
(\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence such that
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h_{n}}\in\Gamma_{M}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h_{n}}\to x\in C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
as
n\to\infty
. Using the compactness of
S^{M}
, we can extract a subsequence of
(h_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
(without re-labelling) such that
h_{n}\rightharpoonup h\in S^{M}
. The linear map
h\mapsto\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}\in C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
is weak-strong continuous by Lemma
D.2
and the compactness of the embedding
L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap W^{1,2}_{T}\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\subset C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
.
Therefore
x=\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}\in\Gamma_{M}
, which shows that
\Gamma_{M}
is closed hence compact in
C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, proving
1
.

Next we show
2
. Let
M<\infty
and
(u^{(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a sequence such that
u^{(\varepsilon)}\in L^{2,M}_{\textnormal{pred}}
for every
\varepsilon>0
and
u^{(\varepsilon)}\rightharpoonup u
in
S^{M}
in law as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Recall that
u^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}=u^{(\varepsilon)}\ast\psi_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
. An application of the Skorokhod representation theorem
[
EK86
, Chpt. 3, Thm. 1.8]
yields a probability space
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})
that supports a sequence of random variables
(\tilde{u}^{(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
and
\tilde{u}
such that
\text{Law}(\tilde{u}^{(\varepsilon)})=\text{Law}(u^{(\varepsilon)})
for every
\varepsilon>0
,
\text{Law}(\tilde{u})=\text{Law}(u)
and
\tilde{u}^{(\varepsilon)}\rightharpoonup\tilde{u}
in
S^{M}
almost surely as
\varepsilon\to 0
. It then follows by classical continuity properties of the weak dual pairing
[
Bre11
, Prop. 3.5]
that for every
\phi\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

\langle\phi,\tilde{u}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}\rangle_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})}\to\langle\phi,\tilde{u}\rangle_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})}\quad\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\text{-almost surely},

which can be used to show that
\tilde{u}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}\rightharpoonup\tilde{u}
in
S^{M}
in law and, therefore,
u^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}\rightharpoonup u
in
S^{M}
in law as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
This combined with the weak-strong continuity of
S^{M}\ni h\to\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}\in\Gamma_{M}
and the continuous mapping theorem yields
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{u^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}}\to\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{u}
in
C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
in law.

Proposition
E.4
and the relative scaling
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta(\varepsilon)^{-\gamma-2}\to 0
yield
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\to 0\in C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{P})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
for every
p\in[1,\infty]
, which implies convergence in probability by Markov’s inequality. Therefore we can deduce by Slutsky’s theorem that
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}+\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{u^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}}\to\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{u}
in
C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
in law, which shows
2
.

Having shown
1
and
2
, we can apply
[
BDM08
, Thm. 6]
to deduce a large deviation principle for
(\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
in
C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
with good rate function
\mathscr{I}_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}}
.
∎

We can now apply the contraction principle and Lemma
3.1
to deduce a large deviation principle for solutions to (
1.8
) in
(\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
under the relative scaling
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta(\varepsilon)^{-\gamma-2}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
for some
\gamma\in(-1/2,0)

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\overline{\rho_{0}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
2.1
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence (see Lemma
2.4
). Assume
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta(\varepsilon)^{-\gamma-2}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
for some
\gamma\in(-1/2,0)
. Then for all
T<T^{*}
, it follows that the sequence
(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
satisfies a large deviation principle in
(\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
with speed
\varepsilon
and good rate function

\mathscr{I}(\rho)\coloneq\inf\Bigl\{\frac{1}{2}\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}:\rho=P\rho_{0}-\chi\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[\rho\nabla\Phi_{\rho}]-\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}h]\Bigr\}.

Proof.

The solution map constructed in Lemma
3.1
is locally Lipschitz continuous in the noise
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\in C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, hence we can apply Lemma
3.4
and the contraction principle
[
DZ10
, Thm. 4.2.1]
to deduce that
(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
satisfies a large deviation principle in
(\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
with speed
\varepsilon
and good rate function
\mathscr{I}
.
∎

3.4
Controlling Negative Values

The solution
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
to (
1.8
) is an additive-noise approximation to the Keller–Segel–Dean–Kawasaki dynamics (
1.1
) which models the density fluctuations of the chemotactic particle system (
1.2
). However, while densities are assumed to be non-negative,
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
can produce negative values due to the additive nature of the noise. The goal of this subsection is to control the probability of the event that
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
produces negative values.

Let
T^{*}
be the maximal time of existence for
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
(see Lemma
2.4
),
T<T^{*}
,
L>0
and denote by

S_{L}\coloneq T\wedge L\wedge\inf\Bigl\{t\in[0,T]:\lVert\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}(t)\rVert_{L^{2}}>L\Bigr\}

(3.2)

the first time that the
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
-norm of
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
exceeds the level
L
. Denote the negative part of
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
by
u^{-}\coloneq\min\{0,u\}
. Given a tolerance
\lambda>0
we show that the event
\{\lVert(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})^{-}\rVert_{C_{S_{L}}L^{2}}\geq\lambda\}
has exponentially small probability in
\varepsilon^{-1}(1+\delta(\varepsilon)^{-2})^{-2}
asymptotically as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\overline{\rho_{0}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
2.1
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence (see Lemma
2.4
). Assume
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta(\varepsilon)^{-2}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Then for all
T<T^{*}
,
L>0
and
\lambda>0
, there exists some
\mu>0
such that

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon(1+\delta(\varepsilon)^{-2})^{2}\log\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\lVert(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})^{-}\rVert_{C_{S_{L}}L^{2}}\geq\lambda\Bigr)\lesssim-\mu^{2}\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\rVert_{C_{T}L^{2}\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}}^{-2}.

Proof.

For every
L>0
let
S_{L}
be given by (
3.2
). By combining the non-negativity of
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
(Theorem
2.2
) with the Lipschitz continuity of
u\mapsto u^{-}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})

we obtain for every
S\in[0,T]
,

\lVert(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})^{-}\rVert_{C_{S}L^{2}}=\lVert(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})^{-}-\rho_{\textnormal{det}}^{-}\rVert_{C_{S}L^{2}}\leq\lVert\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}-\rho_{\textnormal{det}}\rVert_{C_{S}L^{2}},

which implies for every
\lambda>0
,

\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\lVert(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})^{-}\rVert_{C_{S_{L}}L^{2}}\geq\lambda\Bigr)\leq\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\lVert\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}-\rho_{\textnormal{det}}\rVert_{C_{S_{L}}L^{2}}\geq\lambda\Bigr).

(3.3)

Further, using the explicit form of the metric
D_{T}^{L^{2}}
on
(L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
(see Subsection
1.3
and
[
Cha+22
, Sec. 1.5.1]
) we deduce

\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\lVert\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}-\rho_{\textnormal{det}}\rVert_{C_{S_{L}}L^{2}}\geq\lambda\Bigr)\leq\mathbb{P}\Bigl(D_{T}^{L^{2}}(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)},\rho_{\textnormal{det}})\geq\widetilde{\lambda}\Bigr)

(3.4)

where
\widetilde{\lambda}>0
depends on
\lambda
and
\lVert\rho_{\textnormal{det}}\rVert_{C_{T}L^{2}}
.
Using the (local Lipschitz) continuity of the solution
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
in the noise
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}
(Lemma
3.1
), we deduce that there exists some
\mu>0
such that

\Bigl\{D_{T}^{L^{2}}(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)},\rho_{\textnormal{det}})\geq\widetilde{\lambda}\Bigr\}\subset\Bigl\{\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\rVert_{C_{T}L^{2}\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}}\geq\mu\Bigr\}

and consequently,

\mathbb{P}\Bigl(D_{T}^{L^{2}}(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)},\rho_{\textnormal{det}})\geq\widetilde{\lambda}\Bigr)\leq\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\rVert_{C_{T}L^{2}\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}}\geq\mu\Bigr).

(3.5)

Combining the relative scaling
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\delta(\varepsilon)^{-2}\to 0
with Proposition
E.4
we can show that there exists some
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for every
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
it holds that
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathbb{E}[\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\rVert_{C_{T}L^{2}\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}}]\leq\mu/2
.
Denote
E\coloneq C_{T}L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
; we can now apply Lemma
E.5
to bound for every
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
,

\begin{split}&\varepsilon(1+\delta(\varepsilon)^{-2})^{2}\log\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\rVert_{E}\geq\mu\Bigr)\\
&=\varepsilon(1+\delta(\varepsilon)^{-2})^{2}\log\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\rVert_{E}-\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathbb{E}[\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\rVert_{E}]\geq\mu-\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathbb{E}[\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\rVert_{E}]\Bigr)\\
&=\varepsilon(1+\delta(\varepsilon)^{-2})^{2}\log\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\rVert_{E}-\mathbb{E}[\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\rVert_{E}]\geq\varepsilon^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mu-\mathbb{E}[\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\rVert_{E}]\Bigr)\\
&\lesssim-\varepsilon\Bigl(\varepsilon^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mu-\mathbb{E}[\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\rVert_{E}]\Bigr)^{2}\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\rVert_{E}^{-2}\\
&=-\Bigl(\mu-\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathbb{E}[\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\rVert_{E}]\Bigr)^{2}\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\rVert_{E}^{-2}\\
&\lesssim-\mu^{2}\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\rVert_{C_{T}L^{2}\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}}^{-2},\end{split}

(3.6)

where in the application of Lemma
E.5
we used that
\varepsilon^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mu-\mathbb{E}[\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\rVert_{E}]>0
for every
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
.
Combining (
3.3
), (
3.4
), (
3.5
) and (
3.6
), we arrive at

\begin{split}&\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon(1+\delta(\varepsilon)^{-2})^{2}\log\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\lVert(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})^{-}\rVert_{C_{S_{L}}L^{2}}\geq\lambda\Bigr)\lesssim-\mu^{2}\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\rVert_{C_{T}L^{2}\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}}^{-2},\end{split}

which yields the claim.
∎

4
Rough Theory for Large Noise Intensities

In this section we first show the well-posedness of the solution
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
to (
1.8
) in the Hölder–Besov space
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
of regularity
\alpha+1<-1
(Theorem
4.2
), which we then use to establish a law of large numbers (Theorem
4.4
) and a large deviation principle (Theorem
4.8
) under the relative scaling
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Furthermore, we also establish a central limit theorem under the scaling
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
(Theorem
4.6
).

Throughout this section we consider initial data
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\overline{\rho_{0}}=1
, which ensures that
\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\in C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
(see Lemma
2.6
).

4.1
Well-Posedness

In this subsection we use the arguments of
[
MM25
]
to show that (
1.8
) is well-posed in a space of Hölder–Besov distributions
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
of regularity
\alpha+1<-1
(Theorem
4.2
). Our technique relies on the paracontrolled decomposition
[
GIP15
]
(cf. (
4.2
)), which, compared to the mild formulation (
1.8
), introduces more structure to achieve the well-posedness of (
1.8
) in a larger space.

The paracontrolled decomposition of (
1.8
) has been carried out in
[
MM25
, Subsec. 1.2]
for the equation with generic heterogeneity
\sigma\in C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
driven by space-time white noise (correlation length zero; suitably renormalised). Here, we recap the decomposition for positive correlation lengths
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon)>0
without renormalisation and further specialize the heterogeneity to
\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\in C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
(see Lemma
2.6
).

We first apply the so-called Da Prato–Debussche trick and consider the remainder
u\coloneq\rho+\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}
, which then satisfies the equation

u=P\rho_{0}-\chi\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[u\nabla\Phi_{u}]+\chi\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[u\nabla\Phi_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}}]+\chi\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}\nabla\Phi_{u}]-\chi\varepsilon\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}\nabla\Phi_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}}].

The regularity of
u
is consequently determined by the second-order stochastic object given by
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{can}}\coloneq\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}\nabla\Phi_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}}]
. While this improves the situation, this is not yet enough to make sense of the equation under the assumption
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}\in C_{T}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, so we apply the Da Prato–Debussche trick again and define the remainder
w\coloneq u+\chi\varepsilon\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{can}}
. This is still not enough and subsequent applications of the Da Prato–Debussche trick no longer yield any improvements.

To proceed further, we apply the paracontrolled Ansatz of
[
GIP15
]
, which allows us to make sense of (
1.8
) in a space of distributions, if we can define a vector of stochastic objects
\mathbb{X}_{\delta}^{(\varepsilon)}=(\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta},\varepsilon\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}_{\textnormal{can}}^{\delta},\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}_{\textnormal{can}}^{\delta},\varepsilon\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta})
that satisfy the relations

\begin{split}&\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}\coloneq\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\bm{\xi}^{\delta}],\quad\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}_{\textnormal{can}}^{\delta}\coloneq\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}\nabla\Phi_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}}],\\
&\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}_{\textnormal{can}}^{\delta}\coloneq\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}_{\textnormal{can}}^{\delta}\mathbin{\varodot}\nabla\Phi_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}}+\nabla\Phi_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}_{\textnormal{can}}^{\delta}}\mathbin{\varodot}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta},\quad\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}\coloneq\nabla\mathcal{I}[\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}]\mathbin{\varodot}\nabla\Phi_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}}+\nabla^{2}\mathcal{I}[\Phi_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}}]\mathbin{\varodot}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta},\end{split}

(4.1)

and are suitably regular. Recall that
\mathbin{\varodot}
denotes the resonant product, see e.g. Subsection
1.3
or
[
GIP15
]
for a definition. We call this vector the (canonical)
enhancement
. For the existence and regularity of
\mathbb{X}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta}
, see Theorem
4.1
.

The fully decomposed equation and its solution take the following form (cf.
[
MM25
, Subsec. 1.2]
): We call
\bm{w}=(w,w^{\prime},w^{\#})
a paracontrolled triple to (
1.8
), if

\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} w&\coloneq\chi\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[w^{\prime}\mathbin{\varolessthan}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}]+w^{\#},\quad w^{\prime}\coloneq\nabla\Phi_{w}-\chi\varepsilon\nabla\Phi_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{can}}},\\

(4.2)

with

\begin{split}\Omega^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta}(\bm{w})&\coloneq-w\nabla\Phi_{w}+\chi\varepsilon(w\nabla\Phi_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{can}}})+\chi\varepsilon(\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{can}}\nabla\Phi_{w})-\chi^{2}\varepsilon^{2}(\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{can}}\nabla\Phi_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{can}}})-\chi\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}_{\te where
\mathscr{C}(f,g,h)
denotes the commutator
(f\mathbin{\varolessthan}g)\mathbin{\varodot}h-f(g\mathbin{\varodot}h)
of
[
GIP15
, Lem. 2.4]
. We frequently denote
w^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta}=w
to point out the dependence of
