on
(\varepsilon,\delta)
. We call
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta}\coloneq-\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}-\chi\varepsilon\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{can}}+w^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta}
the paracontrolled solution to (
1.8
). Since the correlation length is non-zero, it follows that
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta}
solves (
1.8
) in the classical sense.

The next theorem recaps the existence and regularity of the noise enhancement
\mathbb{X}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta}
in
\mathcal{X}^{\alpha,\kappa}_{T}
(cf. Subsection
1.3
) along with its blow-up as
\delta\to 0
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\overline{\rho_{0}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
2.1
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
,
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence (see Theorem
2.2
),
T<T^{*}
,
\bm{\xi}
be a two-dimensional vector of space-time white noises,
(\psi_{\delta})_{\delta>0}
be a sequence of mollifiers as in (
1.14
),
\bm{\xi}^{\delta}\coloneq\psi_{\delta}\ast\bm{\xi}\coloneq(\psi_{\delta}\ast\xi^{1},\psi_{\delta}\ast\xi^{2})
and
\mathbb{X}_{\delta}^{(\varepsilon)}=(\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta},\varepsilon\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}_{\textnormal{can}}^{\delta},\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}_{\textnormal{can}}^{\delta},\varepsilon\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta})
be given by (
4.1
) for every
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta>0
. Then for all
\alpha\in(-5/2,-2)
,
\kappa\in(0,1/2)
and
p\in[1,\infty)
, it holds that

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}[\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}}^{p}]^{1/p}\lesssim\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\rVert_{C_{T}L^{\infty}},\quad\mathbb{E}[\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{can}}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{C}^{2\alpha+4}}^{p}]^{1/p}\lesssim(1\vee\log(\delta^{-1}))\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\rVert_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{2}}^{2},\\
&\mathbb{E}[\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{can}}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{C}^{3\alpha+6}}^{p}]^{1/p}\lesssim(1\vee\log(\delta^{-1}))\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\rVert_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{2}}^{3},\quad\mathbb{E}[\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{C}^{2\alpha+4}}^{p}]^{1/p}\lesssim\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\rVert_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{2}}^{2},\end{split}

and in particular
\mathbb{X}_{\delta}^{(\varepsilon)}\in\mathcal{X}^{\alpha,\kappa}_{T}
almost surely.

Proof.

The proof follows by
[
MM25
, Thm. 2.3, Lem. 2.7, Lem. 2.20 & Lem. 2.25]
with the particular choice of heterogeneity
\sigma=\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\in C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
(Lemma
2.6
).
∎

The next theorem establishes the well-posedness of paracontrolled solutions to (
1.8
) (cf.
[
MM25
, Def. 3.10]
).

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\overline{\rho_{0}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
2.1
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence (see Theorem
2.2
). Then for all
T<T^{*}
,
\varepsilon>0
,
\delta>0
and
\alpha\in(-9/4,-2)
there exists a unique (paracontrolled) solution
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta}
to (
1.8
) such that
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta}\in(\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
almost surely. Furthermore, the solution map is locally Lipschitz continuous in the noise enhancement
\mathbb{X}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta}
.

Proof.

The proof follows by Theorem
4.1
and
[
MM25
, Lem. 3.20]
, where we used that the arguments of
[
MM25
]
are agnostic as to changes in
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
. For the local Lipschitz continuity in the noise enhancement, see
[
MM25
, Lem. 3.13]
.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

Following
[
MM25
, Rem. 2.4]
, it should be possible to consider more general initial data
\rho_{0}
such that
(1-\Delta)\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\in C_{T}L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
(modulo the introduction of a suitable weight at
0
to solve (
1.8
), cf.
[
MM25
, Lem. 3.20]
.) This would suggest taking initial data in the Bessel potential space
\mathcal{H}^{2}_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
of regularity
2
and integrability
1
. Unfortunately,
\mathcal{H}^{2}_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
seems to be highly pathological (see
[
Ste70
, Chpt. V, Ex. 6.6]
for a discussion in the full space) and it is not even clear if one can establish
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}\in C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{2}_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
. A suitable, albeit suboptimal, replacement is given by
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{B}_{1,1}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, which allows us to solve
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}\in C_{T}\mathcal{B}_{1,1}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
. A generalization of Lemma
2.6
would then imply
\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\in C_{T}\mathcal{B}_{1,1}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, so that in particular
(1-\Delta)\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\in C_{T}\mathcal{B}_{1,1}^{0}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\hookrightarrow C_{T}L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
. However, this extension doesn’t seem sufficiently interesting to warrant an extensive discussion.

4.2
Law of Large Numbers

In this subsection we establish a (weak) law of large numbers for solutions
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
to (
1.8
) in
(\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
for every
\alpha\in(-9/4,-2)
under the relative scaling
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
(Theorem
4.4
). See
[
GIP15
, Cor. 4.7]
and
[
CC18
, Cor. 3.13]
for similar results in the literature.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\overline{\rho_{0}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
2.1
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence (see Theorem
2.2
). Assume
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Then for all
T<T^{*}
and
\alpha\in(-9/4,-2)
, it follows that
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}\to\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
in
(\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
in probability as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Proof.

It follows by the relative scaling
\varepsilon\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})\to 0
, Theorem
4.1
and Markov’s inequality that
\mathbb{X}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}\to 0
in
\mathcal{X}^{\alpha,\kappa}_{T}
in probability for every
\alpha\in(-5/2,-2)
and
\kappa\in(0,1/2)
. Further for every
\alpha\in(-9/4,-2)
we can apply Theorem
4.2
to construct the paracontrolled solution
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
to (
1.8
) such that
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}\in(\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
almost surely. Using that
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
is locally Lipschitz continuous in
\mathbb{X}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
(Theorem
4.2
), we can apply the continuous mapping theorem to deduce
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}\to\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
in
(\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
in probability.
∎

4.3
Central Limit Theorem

Having established a law of large numbers (Theorem
4.4
), we now turn to a central limit theorem (Theorem
4.6
). For this we need to specialize our relative scaling to
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
since we lose one order of
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
on the scale of the leading-order fluctuations
\varepsilon^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}-\rho_{\textnormal{det}})
.

We first show the well-posedness of the generalized Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process, which describes the limiting dynamics of the leading-order fluctuations.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\overline{\rho_{0}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
2.1
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence (see Theorem
2.2
). Then for all
T<T^{*}
and
\alpha\in(-3,-2)
there exists a unique mild solution
v\in C_{T}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
to

\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} (\partial_{t}-\Delta)v&=-\chi\nabla\cdot(v\nabla\Phi_{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}})-\chi\nabla\cdot(\rho_{\textnormal{det}}\nabla\Phi_{v})-\nabla\cdot(\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\bm{\xi}),\\
v|_{t=0}&=0.\end{aligned}\end{cases}

(4.3)

We refer to this
v
as the generalized Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process.

Proof.

It suffices to construct a fixed point
u
to the linear mild equation

u=-\chi\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[u\nabla\Phi_{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}]-\chi\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[\rho_{\textnormal{det}}\nabla\Phi_{u}]+\chi\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}\nabla\Phi_{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}]+\chi\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[\rho_{\textnormal{det}}\nabla\Phi_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}}],

(4.4)

from which we may recover a suitable solution
v
to (
4.3
) via the Da Prato–Debussche decomposition
v=-\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}+u
. In particular, the space regularity of
v
is determined by the regularity of
which is given by
C_{T}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
(cf.
[
MM25
, Lem. 2.7]
). It follows by the linearity of (
4.4
) that the lifetimes of
v
and
u
are determined by the lifetimes of their coefficients. The lower bound on
\alpha
ensures that the products
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}\nabla\Phi_{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}
and
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}\nabla\Phi_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}}
appearing in (
4.4
) are well-posed by Bony’s estimates
[
GIP15
, Lem. 2.1]
.
∎

Next we show the central limit theorem for
(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
.

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\overline{\rho_{0}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
2.1
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence (see Theorem
2.2
). Assume
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Then for all
T<T^{*}
and
\alpha\in(-9/4,-2)
, it follows that
\varepsilon^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}-\rho_{\textnormal{det}})\to v
in
(\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
in probability as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Proof.

We use the Da Prato–Debussche decompositions for
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
and
v
, given by
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}=-\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}-\chi\varepsilon\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}_{\textnormal{can}}+w^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
(see Subsection
4.1
) and
v=-\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}+u
(see the proof of Lemma
4.5
), to deduce

\varepsilon^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}-\rho_{\textnormal{det}})-v=\varepsilon^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(w^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}-\rho_{\textnormal{det}})-u-\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}+\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}-\chi\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}_{\textnormal{can}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}.

The convergence
\varepsilon^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}(w^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}-\rho_{\textnormal{det}})\to u
follows by the paracontrolled decomposition, in particular the local Lipschitz continuity of the paracontrolled solution in the enhancement (Theorem
4.2
); the convergence
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\to\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}
follows by
[
MM25
, Lem. 2.7]
; the convergence
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}_{\textnormal{can}}\to 0
follows by Theorem
4.1
combined with the relative scaling
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})\to 0
.
∎

4.4
Large Deviation Principle

In this subsection we apply a generalization of Freidlin and Wentzell’s theory (see Appendix
C
as well as
[
HW15
]
) to establish a large deviation principle for solutions
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
to (
1.8
) in
(\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
for every
\alpha\in(-9/4,-2)
under the relative scaling
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
(Theorem
4.8
). As in Subsection
4.2
we first consider the noise enhancement
(\mathbb{X}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
driving
(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
(cf. Theorem
4.2
) for which we establish a large deviation principle (Theorem
4.7
) that we can then transfer to
(\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
via the contraction principle (Theorem
4.8
).

Recall that
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
denotes the space of vector-valued space-time Hölder distributions of regularity
\alpha
equipped with the parabolic scaling
\mathfrak{s}=(2,1,1)
(see Definition
A.17
).

Theorem 4.7
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\overline{\rho_{0}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
2.1
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence (see Theorem
2.2
). Assume
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Then for all
T<T^{*}
,
\alpha\in(-5/2,-2)
and
\kappa\in(0,1/2)
, it follows that the sequence
(\mathbb{X}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
satisfies a large deviation principle in
\mathcal{X}^{\alpha,\kappa}_{T}
(cf. Subsection
1.3
) with speed
\varepsilon
and good rate function

\mathscr{I}_{\textnormal{Enh}}(\bm{s})\coloneq\inf\Bigl\{\frac{1}{2}\lVert h\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})}:\mathbb{X}^{h}=\bm{s}\Bigr\},

where
\mathbb{X}^{h}
is the enhancement driven by the Cameron–Martin element
h\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
defined as in (
D.3
) (see also Lemma
D.3
.)

Proof.

It follows by the Wiener chaos decomposition
[
MM25
, Subsec. 2.2]
, Theorem
B.3
and Lemma
B.5
that every enhancement
\mathbb{X}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}^{(\varepsilon)}=(\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)},\varepsilon\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}_{\textnormal{can}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)},\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}_{\textnormal{can}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)},\varepsilon\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)})
is of the form (
C.3
) with abstract Wiener space

B\coloneq\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2}),\qquad\mu\coloneq\textnormal{Law}(\bm{\xi}),\qquad\mathcal{H}_{\mu}\coloneq L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2}),

index set

\mathcal{W}\coloneq\{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}},\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}},\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}},\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}\},\qquad(K_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}},K_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}},K_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}},K_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}})\coloneq(1,2,3,2)

and target space

\mathbf{E}\coloneq\bigoplus_{\tau\in\mathcal{W}}E_{\tau}\coloneq\mathscr{L}^{\kappa}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})\times\mathscr{L}^{\kappa}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{2\alpha+4}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})\times\mathscr{L}^{\kappa}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{3\alpha+6}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathscr{L}^{\kappa}_{T}\mathcal{C}^{2\alpha+4}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}),

which is separable by an application of Lemma
A.12
combined with the fact that finite Cartesian products of separable spaces are again separable.

Using the relative scaling
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})\to 0
combined with the bounds
[
MM25
, Lem. 2.7]
(for
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}
),
[
MM25
, Lem. 2.16 & Lem. 2.26]
(for
\varepsilon\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}_{\textnormal{can}}
),
[
MM25
, Lem. 2.19, Lem. 2.24 and Lem. 2.26]
(for
\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}_{\textnormal{can}}
) and
[
MM25
, Lem. 2.20 & Lem. 2.25]
(for
\varepsilon\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}
), one can show that
\mathbb{X}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
converges as
\varepsilon\to 0
in the sense of (
C.4
) to a non-trivial limit
\mathbb{X}=\bigoplus_{\tau\in\mathcal{W}}\mathbb{X}_{\tau,K_{\tau}}
which has no contributions from lower-order Wiener chaoses, (
C.5
).

An application of Theorem
C.4
then implies that
(\mathbb{X}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}^{(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
satisfies a large deviation principle in
\mathbf{E}
with speed
\varepsilon
and good rate function

\mathscr{I}_{\textnormal{Enh}}(\bm{s})\coloneq\inf\Bigl\{\frac{1}{2}\lVert h\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})}:\mathbb{X}_{\hom}(h)=\bm{s}\Bigr\},

where (cf. Definition
C.3
) the homogeneous part
\mathbb{X}_{\hom}(h)
is given by

\mathbb{X}_{\hom}(h)=\bigoplus_{\tau\in\mathcal{W}}(\mathbb{X}_{\tau,K_{\tau}})_{\hom}=\bigoplus_{\tau\in\mathcal{W}}\int_{B}\mathbb{X}_{\tau,K_{\tau}}(\xi+h)\mu(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mu\xi).

We can further apply Lemma
B.6
to identify the homogeneous part
\mathbb{X}_{\hom}(h)
with the enhancement
\mathbb{X}^{h}
driven by the Cameron–Martin element
h
(cf. Appendix
D
), which yields the rate function of the claim.

The large deviation principle on
\mathcal{X}^{\alpha,\kappa}_{T}
then follows by
[
DZ10
, Lem. 4.1.5 (b)]
, using that
\mathcal{X}^{\alpha,\kappa}_{T}\subset\mathbf{E}
is closed (by definition; cf. Subsection
1.3
) and that
\mathbb{X}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}\in\mathcal{X}^{\alpha,\kappa}_{T}
almost surely for every
\varepsilon>0
(Theorem
4.1
).
∎

We can now apply the contraction principle and Theorem
4.7
to deduce a large deviation principle for solutions to (
1.8
) in
(\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
for every
\alpha\in(-9/4,-2)
under the relative scaling
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Theorem 4.8
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\overline{\rho_{0}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
2.1
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence (see Theorem
2.2
). Assume
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Then for all
T<T^{*}
and
\alpha\in(-9/4,-2)
, it follows that
(\rho_{\delta(\varepsilon)}^{(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
satisfies a large deviation principle in
(\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
with speed
\varepsilon
and good rate function

\mathscr{I}(\rho)\coloneq\inf\Bigl\{\frac{1}{2}\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}:\rho=P\rho_{0}-\chi\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[\rho\nabla\Phi_{\rho}]-\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}h]\Bigr\}.

Proof.

An application of Theorem
4.2
shows that
\rho_{\delta(\varepsilon)}^{(\varepsilon)}\in(\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
is locally Lipschitz continuous in
\mathbb{X}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}\in\mathcal{X}^{\alpha,\kappa}_{T}
, which we can combine with the contraction principle
[
DZ10
, Thm. 4.2.1]
and Theorem
4.7
to deduce the claim.
∎

As the following proposition shows, it is possible to remove the relative scaling in Theorem
4.8
, if the initial data
\rho_{0}
is uniform, i.e.
\rho_{0}\equiv 1
.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
\rho_{0}\equiv 1
and
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
2.1
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
. Assume
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Then for all
T>0
and
\alpha\in(-9/4,-2)
, it follows that
(\rho_{\delta(\varepsilon)}^{(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
satisfies a large deviation principle in
(\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
with speed
\varepsilon
and good rate function
\mathscr{I}
.

Proof.

The deterministic Keller–Segel equation (
2.1
) preserves uniform initial data, that is,
\rho_{0}\equiv 1
yields
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}\equiv 1
globally.
In particular,
\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\equiv 1
which by
[
MM25
, Rem. 2.28]
implies that for every
\delta\geq 0
and
p\in[1,\infty)
,

\mathbb{E}[\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{can}}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{C}^{2\alpha+4}}^{p}]^{1/p}\lesssim 1,\qquad\mathbb{E}[\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{\delta}_{\textnormal{can}}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{C}^{3\alpha+6}}^{p}]^{1/p}\lesssim 1.

(4.5)

The relative scaling of Theorem
4.7
(and, consequently, Theorem
4.8
) was chosen such that
\mathbb{X}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
converges as
\varepsilon\to 0
in the sense of (
C.4
) to a limit that has no contributions from lower-order Wiener chaoses, (
C.5
). As demonstrated by (
4.5
), in the uniform case there is no such divergence as
\delta\to 0
, hence we can choose
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
arbitrarily.
∎

Remark 4.10
.

In contrast to Proposition
4.9
, which concerns the large deviations in the rough setting, one cannot improve the relative scaling in the regular setting (Theorem
3.5
) by choosing uniform initial data.

4.5
Controlling Blow-Ups

In this subsection we apply the large deviation principle (Theorem
4.8
) to control the probability that
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
blows up before time
T<T^{*}
(Theorem
4.12
). In particular, we show that the probability of observing a blow-up is asymptotically exponentially small in
\varepsilon^{-1}
, a result that was inspired by
[
HW15
, Rem. 4.6]
.

A.6

which yields the claim.
∎

We can now use Lemma
4.11
to deduce that solutions
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
to (
1.8
) blow up before time
T<T^{*}
with exponentially small probability in
\varepsilon
.

Theorem 4.12
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}>0
and
\overline{\rho_{0}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
2.1
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence (see Theorem
2.2
). Assume
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 0
and
\varepsilon\log(\delta(\varepsilon)^{-1})\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Then for all
S\leq T<T^{*}
and
\alpha\in(-9/4,-2)

c>0

Using the local Lipschitz continuity of the solution map in the noise enhancement (Theorem
4.2
), we deduce that for every
\nu>0
there exists an
R>0
such that
\lVert\mathbb{X}^{h}\rVert_{\mathcal{X}^{\alpha,\kappa}_{T}}<R
implies
D_{T}^{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha+1}}(\rho^{h},\rho_{\textnormal{det}})<\nu
, where
\mathbb{X}^{h}
denotes the enhancement driven by the Cameron–Martin element
h
(cf. Lemma
D.3
). Hence, combining both we deduce that for every
S\leq T
there exists an
R>0
such that

.

By an application of Lemma
D.3
, we can find for every

a constant

.

we can find a constant

Appendix A
Analytic Toolbox

In this appendix we collect general, useful results which are applied throughout. In particular, the results of this appendix are agnostic as to the dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}
, which we keep arbitrary but fixed throughout. The definitions introduced in Subsection
1.3
generalize to
d
dimensions
mutatis mutandis
.

A.1
Bounds and Embeddings

We apply the following Sobolev and Gagliardo–Nirenberg–Sobolev inequalities in the two-dimensional case to prove Theorem
2.2
.

Lemma A.1

(Sobolev’s inequality)
.

The following hold:

1.

Let
s>0
and
2<q<\infty
be such that
\frac{s}{d}\geq\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{q}
; then
\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\hookrightarrow L^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and

\lVert u\rVert_{L^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

(A.1)

2.

Let
s>d/2
; then
\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\hookrightarrow C(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and

\lVert u\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

(A.2)

Proof.

See
[
BO13
, Cor. 1.2 & Subsec. 2.1]
.
∎

A combination of Sobolev’s inequality (Lemma
A.1
) with the Gagliardo–Nirenberg interpolation estimate
[
BM18
]
yields the following Gagliardo–Nirenberg–Sobolev inequality, see
[
BM19
]
.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
q,r\in[1,\infty]
; then given
u\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\cap L^{r}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that
\overline{u}=0
and
\theta\in(0,1)
satisfying

\theta\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{d}\Bigl)+\frac{1-\theta}{r}=\frac{1}{q},

there exists a constant
C\coloneq C(d,q,r)>0
such that

\lVert u\rVert_{L^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leq C\lVert\nabla u\rVert^{\theta}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\lVert u\rVert^{1-\theta}_{L^{r}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

(A.3)

We define
C_{\textnormal{gns-per}}(d,q,r)
to be the optimal constant such that (
A.3
) holds.

Proof.

The result follows by an application of
[
BM19
, Cor. 1]
upon passing between
(0,1)^{2}
and
\mathbb{T}^{2}
.
∎

Recall from Subsection
1.3
the notation for the heat semigroup,

P_{t}f=\mathscr{F}^{-1}(\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-t\lvert 2\pi\,\cdot\,\rvert^{2}}\widehat{f}(\,\cdot\,)),\qquad\text{for every}\penalty\ t\geq 0,\,f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2})

and the resolution of the heat equation,

\mathcal{I}[f]_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}P_{t-s}f_{s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus,\qquad\text{for every}\penalty\ T>0,\,t\in[0,T],\,f\colon[0,T]\to\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2}).

The following Schauder estimates are applied in the proof of Lemma
3.1
.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
T>0
and
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, then the following hold:

1.

The map
P\colon\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is continuous and

\lVert Pf\rVert_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}\cap L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}}\lesssim_{T}\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}}.

2.

If
\gamma^{\prime}\leq\gamma<\gamma^{\prime}+2
, then the map
\mathcal{I}\colon C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is continuous and

\lVert\mathcal{I}[f]\rVert_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}}\lesssim(T+T^{1-\frac{\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}}{2}})\lVert f\rVert_{C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma^{\prime}}}.

3.

If
\gamma^{\prime}\leq\gamma\leq\gamma^{\prime}+2
, then the map
\mathcal{I}\colon L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is continuous and

\lVert\mathcal{I}[f]\rVert_{L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}}\lesssim(T+T^{1-\frac{\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}}{2}})\lVert f\rVert_{L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma^{\prime}}}.

Proof.

To show that
P
and
\mathcal{I}
are continuous maps into
C_{T}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, it suffices to argue as in the proof of
[
MM25
, Lem. A.6]
. To establish their regularities in
L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma+1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and
L_{T}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
respectively, one can use the Fourier transform (in space), Young’s convolution inequality and interpolation.
∎

Let
u\in C^{2}_{1}((0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})\cap C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
be the classical solution (if it exists) to the general diffusion-advection equation

\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} (\partial_{t}-\Delta)u&=\nabla\cdot(bu),\quad&&\text{in}\penalty\ (0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\\
u|_{t=0}&=u_{0},\quad&&\text{on}\penalty\ \mathbb{T}^{d},\end{aligned}\end{cases}

(A.4)

with positive initial data
u_{0}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and advection
b\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfying
\nabla\cdot b\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{R})
. In the next proposition we show that
u
stays bounded away from zero, which is used to prove Lemma
2.5
(where existence of a solution is guaranteed.)

Proposition A.4
.

Let
u_{0}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{d})
be such that
u_{0}>0
and
b\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
be such that
\nabla\cdot b\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{R})
. Let
u\in C^{2}_{1}((0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})\cap C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
be any classical solution to (
A.4
). Then there exists a constant
c>0
such that
u(t,x)\geq c
for all
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
.

Proof.

Using the product rule and passing between the torus and the full space,
we find that
u\in C^{2}_{1}((0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap C([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a periodic solution to

\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}u&=\Delta u-uV+b\cdot\nabla u,\quad&&\text{in}\penalty\ (0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
u|_{t=0}&=u_{0},\quad&&\text{on}\penalty\ \mathbb{R}^{d},\end{aligned}\end{cases}

where we denote
V\coloneq-\nabla\cdot b
and abuse notation to identify functions on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
with periodic functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Using the assumption
\nabla\cdot b\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
, it follows that
V\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\lVert V\rVert_{C([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty
.

Let
t\in[0,T]
and for every
v\colon[0,t]\to\mathbb{R}
define the time reversal
\tilde{v}(s)\coloneq v(t-s)
. It follows by
[
RW00
, Chpt. V, Thm. 23.5 & Thm. 20.1]
that for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
there exists a weak solution
((Y_{s})_{s\geq 0},(\mathcal{F}_{s})_{s\geq 0})
to the SDE,

\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} \mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muY_{s}&=\tilde{b}(s,Y_{s})\mathds{1}_{s\leq t}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus+\sqrt{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muB_{s},\\
Y_{0}&=x,\end{aligned}\end{cases}

where we used
b\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
to ensure that
(s,y)\mapsto\tilde{b}(s,y)\mathds{1}_{s\leq t}
is a previsible path functional in the sense of
[
RW00
, Chpt. V, Def. 8.3]
and bounded in a neighbourhood of time
t
. By an application of
[
EK86
, Chpt. 8, Thm. 1.7 & Chpt. 4, Cor. 4.3]
, this solution is unique in law.

We denote
A_{s}\coloneq\int_{0}^{s}\tilde{V}(r,Y_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mur
and
X_{s}\coloneq\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-A_{s}}\tilde{u}(s,Y_{s})
, where
\tilde{u}(s)\coloneq u(t-s)
solves

\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} -\partial_{s}\tilde{u}&=\Delta\tilde{u}-\tilde{u}\tilde{V}+\tilde{b}\cdot\nabla\tilde{u},\quad&&\text{in}\penalty\ [0,t)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
\tilde{u}|_{s=t}&=u_{0},\quad&&\text{on}\penalty\ \mathbb{R}^{d}.\end{aligned}\end{cases}

It then follows by Itô’s formula (cf.
[
KS91
, Chpt. 3, Thm. 3.6]
) that for every
s<t
,

\begin{split}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muX_{s}&=-\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-A_{s}}\tilde{u}(s,Y_{s})\tilde{V}(s,Y_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus+\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-A_{s}}\partial_{s}\tilde{u}(s,Y_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus+\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-A_{s}}\nabla\tilde{u}(s,Y_{s})\cdot\tilde{b}(s,y_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus\\
&\quad+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-A_{s}}\nabla\tilde{u}(s,Y_{s})\cdot\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muB_{s}+\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-A_{s}}\Delta\tilde{u}(s,Y_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus\\
&=\sqrt{2}\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-A_{s}}\nabla\tilde{u}(s,Y_{s})\cdot\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muB_{s},\end{split}

which implies that
(X_{s})_{s\in[0,t)}
is a continuous local martingale up to time
t
.

Let
(T_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a localizing sequence, i.e. each
T_{k}
is a stopping time,
T_{k}<T_{k+1}
,
t=\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}T_{k}
and
(X_{s\wedge T_{k}})_{s\geq 0}
is a martingale for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
. It follows that
X_{s\wedge T_{k}}\to X_{s\wedge t}
almost surely as
k\to\infty
. Furthermore,

\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\lvert X_{s}\rvert\Bigr]\leq\lVert u\rVert_{C([0,t]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{t\lVert V\rVert_{C([0,t]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}}<\infty

and each
\lvert X_{s\wedge T_{k}}\rvert
is dominated by
\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\lvert X_{s}\rvert
, which is integrable by the above. Therefore we obtain by the dominated convergence theorem for all
0\leq r<s<\infty
,

\mathbb{E}[X_{s\wedge t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{r}]\leftarrow\mathbb{E}[X_{s\wedge T_{k}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{r}]=X_{r\wedge T_{k}}\to X_{r\wedge t},

so that
(X_{s\wedge t})_{s\geq 0}
is a martingale.

We can then apply the martingale property to deduce

u(t,x)=\tilde{u}(0,x)=\mathbb{E}[X_{0}]=\mathbb{E}[X_{t}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-A_{t}}u(0,Y_{t})]=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{V}(r,Y_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mur}u_{0}(Y_{t})\Bigr].

Using that
\mathbb{T}^{d}
is compact, it follows by the extreme value theorem that there exists a constant
c_{0}>0
such that
u_{0}>c_{0}
. Therefore for all
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

u(t,x)=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{V}(r,Y_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mur}u_{0}(Y_{t})\Bigr]>c_{0}\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-t\lVert V\rVert_{C([0,t]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}}\geq c_{0}\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-T\lVert V\rVert_{C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})}}>0,

which yields the claim.
∎

We use the following chain rule in Bessel potential spaces in the proof of Lemma
2.6
.

Lemma A.5
.

Let
F\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})
,
\gamma\geq 0
,
c>0
and
u\in\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
be such that
u\geq c
. Then there exists a constant
C(\gamma,F,\lVert u\rVert_{L^{\infty}},c^{-1})\geq 0
satisfying

\lVert F(u)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}}\leq C(\gamma,F,\lVert u\rVert_{L^{\infty}},c^{-1})\lVert u\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}}.

For all
\gamma
and
F
, the function
(x,y)\mapsto C(\gamma,F,x,y)
can be chosen to be non-decreasing.

Proof.

Since
u
is bounded away from
0
, we may use a smooth cut-off to assume without limitation of generality that
F
vanishes at
0
and that
F^{\prime}
is locally bounded.
If
\gamma=0
, this yields the square integrability of
F(u)
.
If
\gamma>0
we may apply
[
BCD11
, Thm. 2.87]
to deduce the claim, see also
[
Con+20
, Lem. B.2]
.
∎

). We show that the map

is lower semicontinuous.

Lemma A.6
.

The map

F^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}\to[0,T]
is lower semicontinuous.

Proof.

We define for every
L>0
and
f\in F^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
the exceedance time
T^{L}_{F}[f]\coloneq T\wedge L\wedge\inf\{t\in[0,T]:\lVert f(t)\rVert_{F}\geq L\}
. Let
S\in[0,T]
, we first show that the sublevel set

(T^{L}_{F})^{-1}([0,S])\coloneq\{f\in F^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}:T^{L}_{F}[f]\leq S\}

is closed in
F^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
, which is equivalent to the lower semicontinuity of
T^{L}_{F}
.

The case
S\geq T\wedge L
is trivial, since
(T^{L}_{F})^{-1}([0,T\wedge L])=F^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
, hence assume
S<T\wedge L
. Let
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a converging sequence in
(T^{L}_{F})^{-1}([0,S])
with limit
f\in F^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
. Our aim is to show that
f\in(T^{L}_{F})^{-1}([0,S])
, so let’s assume for a contradiction that
T^{L}_{F}[f]>S
. The sequence of exceedance times
(T^{L}_{F}[f_{n}])_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of real numbers in
[0,S]
, hence by the compactness of
[0,S]
we can extract a subsequence that converges (modulo re-labelling) to a limit
\lim_{n\to\infty}T^{L}_{F}[f_{n}]=S^{*}\in[0,S]
. Using the continuity of the norm, we obtain

\lVert f(S^{*})\rVert_{F}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\lVert f(T^{L}_{F}[f_{n}])\rVert_{F}\geq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\lVert f_{n}(T^{L}_{F}[f_{n}])\rVert_{F}-\limsup_{n\to\infty}\lVert f(T^{L}_{F}[f_{n}])-f_{n}(T^{L}_{F}[f_{n}])\rVert_{F}=L,

where we used
f_{n}\in(T^{L}_{F})^{-1}([0,S])
and
S<T\wedge L
to deduce
\lVert f_{n}(T^{L}_{F}[f_{n}])\rVert_{F}=L
and the definition of the metric
D_{T}^{F}
on
F^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
(cf. (
1.11
)) to ensure that the second term vanishes.

Therefore,
\lVert f(S^{*})\rVert_{F}\geq L
and by the definition of
T^{L}_{F}[f]
, we obtain
T^{L}_{F}[f]\leq S^{*}\leq S
. It follows that the limit
f\in F^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
satisfies
T^{L}_{F}[f]\leq S
, hence
f\in(T^{L}_{F})^{-1}([0,S])
, which proves the lower semicontinuity of
T^{L}_{F}\colon F^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}\to[0,T]
.

To deduce the lower semicontinuity of the blow-up time

, we use that
