and the general fact that lower semicontinuity is preserved under taking suprema.
∎

A.2
Analysis of Function Spaces

In this appendix we show that each interpolation space
\mathscr{L}_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and each blow-up space
F^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
(see Subsection
1.3
) is separable (Lemma
A.12
and Lemma
A.13
). We then define a space of space-time Hölder distributions
\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
(Definition
A.17
) and also establish its separability (Lemma
A.18
).

Let
p,q\in[1,\infty]
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. Recall (see Subsection
1.3
) that the Besov space
\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is defined as the closure of
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
under the Besov norm
u\mapsto\lVert u\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}\coloneq\lVert(2^{k\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{k}u\rVert_{L^{p}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{-1}}\rVert_{\ell^{q}}
. We first show the separability of each
\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

Lemma A.7
.

Let
p,q\in[1,\infty]
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. It holds that

\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})=\Bigl\{u\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{d}):\lVert u\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}<\infty,\lim_{j\to\infty}2^{j\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{j}u\rVert_{L^{p}}=0\Bigr\}

(A.5)

and each
\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is separable.

Proof.

We start by proving the characterization (
A.5
).

Assume
u\in\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, we need to show that
\lim_{j\to\infty}2^{j\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{j}u\rVert_{L^{p}}=0
. By definition, there exist a sequence
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
u_{n}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
u_{n}\to u\in\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
as
n\to\infty
. It follows that for each
j\in\mathbb{N}_{-1}
,

2^{j\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{j}u\rVert_{L^{p}}\leq 2^{j\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{j}u-\Delta_{j}u_{n}\rVert_{L^{p}}+2^{j\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{j}u_{n}\rVert_{L^{p}}\leq\lVert u-u_{n}\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}+2^{j\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{j}u_{n}\rVert_{L^{p}}.

Letting
j\to\infty
, we obtain
\limsup_{j\to\infty}2^{j\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{j}u\rVert_{L^{p}}\leq\lVert u-u_{n}\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}
, subsequently letting
n\to\infty
yields
\lim_{j\to\infty}2^{j\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{j}u\rVert_{L^{p}}=0
.

Conversely assume that
u\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
satisfies
\lVert u\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}<\infty
and
\lim_{j\to\infty}2^{j\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{j}u\rVert_{L^{p}}=0
. We need to show that there exists a sequence
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
u_{n}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\lVert u-u_{n}\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. We define
u_{n}\coloneq\sum_{j=-1}^{n-1}\Delta_{j}u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and apply
[
GIP15
, Lem. A.3]
:
For
q=\infty
we deduce
\lVert u-u_{n}\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,\infty}^{\alpha}}\lesssim\sup_{j\geq n}(2^{j\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{j}u\rVert_{L^{p}})
, which vanishes as
n\to 0
, since
\lim_{j\to\infty}2^{j\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{j}u\rVert_{L^{p}}=0
.
For
q<\infty
we deduce
\lVert u-u_{n}\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}^{q}\lesssim\sum_{j=n}^{\infty}2^{jq\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{j}u\rVert_{L^{p}}^{q}
, which vanishes as
n\to 0
, since
\lVert u\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}<\infty
. This yields the characterization (
A.5
).

Next we show the separability of each
\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. If
p<\infty
, let
D
be a countable, dense subset of
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
; and if
p=\infty
, let
D
be a countable, dense subset of
C(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. We define the countable set

H\coloneq\Bigl\{\sum_{j=-1}^{J}\Delta_{j}h_{j}:J\in\mathbb{N}_{-1},\penalty\ h_{j}\in D,\penalty\ j=1,\ldots,J\Bigr\}

and show that
H
is dense in
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\subset\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

Assume first
q=\infty
. Let
u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, then for each
\varepsilon>0
there exists a
J\in\mathbb{N}_{-1}
such that
2^{j\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{j}u\rVert_{L^{p}}<\varepsilon
for every
j>J
. For
j=-1,\ldots,J
, let
h_{j}\in D
be such that
\lVert u-h_{j}\rVert_{L^{p}}<\varepsilon 2^{-j\alpha}
, which combined with Poisson’s summation formula and Young’s convolution inequality yields

\lVert\Delta_{j}u-\Delta_{j}h_{j}\rVert_{L^{p}}\leq(\lVert\mathscr{F}^{-1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{0}\rVert_{L^{1}}\vee\lVert\mathscr{F}^{-1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{-1}\rVert_{L^{1}})\lVert u-h_{j}\rVert_{L^{p}}\lesssim\varepsilon 2^{-j\alpha}.

It then follows that

\Bigl\lVert u-\sum_{j=-1}^{J}\Delta_{j}h_{j}\Bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,\infty}^{\alpha}}\lesssim\sup_{j=-1,\ldots,J}\Bigl(2^{j\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{j}u-\Delta_{j}h_{j}\rVert_{L^{p}}\Bigr)\vee\sup_{j=J+1,\ldots,\infty}\Bigl(2^{j\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{j}u\rVert_{L^{p}}\Bigr)\lesssim\varepsilon,

which shows that
H
is dense in
\mathcal{B}_{p,\infty}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

Assume next
q<\infty
. Let
u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, then for each
\varepsilon>0
there exists a
J\in\mathbb{N}_{-1}
such that
2^{j\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{j}u\rVert_{L^{p}}<\varepsilon 2^{-j}
for every
j>J
. For
j=-1,\ldots,J
, let
h_{j}\in D
be such that
\lVert u-h_{j}\rVert_{L^{p}}<\varepsilon 2^{-j(\alpha+1)}
, which combined with Poisson’s summation formula and Young’s convolution inequality yields

\lVert\Delta_{j}u-\Delta_{j}h_{j}\rVert_{L^{p}}\leq(\lVert\mathscr{F}^{-1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{0}\rVert_{L^{1}}\vee\lVert\mathscr{F}^{-1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varrho_{-1}\rVert_{L^{1}})\lVert u-h_{j}\rVert_{L^{p}}\lesssim\varepsilon 2^{-j(\alpha+1)}.

It then follows that

\Bigl\lVert u-\sum_{j=-1}^{J}\Delta_{j}h_{j}\Bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}^{q}\lesssim\sum_{j=-1}^{J}2^{jq\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{j}u-\Delta_{j}h_{j}\rVert_{L^{p}}^{q}+\sum_{j=J+1}^{\infty}2^{jq\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{j}u\rVert_{L^{p}}^{q}\lesssim\varepsilon^{q}\sum_{j=-1}^{\infty}2^{-jq}\lesssim\varepsilon^{q},

which shows that
H
is dense in
\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. This yields the claim.
∎

The next lemma presents a natural criterion that allows us to find elements of
\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

Lemma A.8
.

Let
p,q\in[1,\infty]
and
\alpha<\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
.

•

If
q\in[1,\infty)
, then each
u\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that
\lVert u\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}<\infty
is an element of
\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

•

If
q=\infty
, then each
u\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that
\lVert u\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,\infty}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}<\infty
is an element of
\mathcal{B}_{p,\infty}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

Proof.

Let
p,q\in[1,\infty]
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. To prove the first claim, let
q<\infty
and
u\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
be such that
\lVert u\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}<\infty
. It follows immediately that
\lim_{j\to\infty}2^{j\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{j}u\rVert_{L^{p}}=0
, hence
u\in\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
by Lemma
A.7
. To prove the second claim, let
q=\infty
,
\alpha<\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
and
u\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
be such that
\lVert u\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,\infty}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}<\infty
. It follows that
\lVert u\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,\infty}^{\alpha}}<\infty
and

2^{j\alpha}\lVert\Delta_{j}u\rVert_{L^{p}}\leq 2^{j(\alpha-\alpha^{\prime})}\lVert u\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,\infty}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\to 0\quad\text{as}\penalty\ j\to\infty,

hence
u\in\mathcal{B}_{p,\infty}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
by Lemma
A.7
. This yields the claim.
∎

The next lemma shows that Besov spaces are compactly embedded upon passing to a larger space.

Lemma A.9
.

Let
p,q\in[1,\infty]
and
\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. It follows that the embedding
\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is compact.

Proof.

The proof follows by similar arguments as in
[
BCD11
, Thm. 2.94]
.
∎

Recall that the space
C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is defined as the completion of
C^{\infty}_{T}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
under the usual Hölder norm for
\kappa\in(0,1)
and that
C_{T}^{0}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\coloneq C_{T}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
(cf. Subsection
1.3
). Next we show that each
C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is separable.

Lemma A.10
.

Let
T>0
,
p,q\in[1,\infty]
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
\kappa\in[0,1)
. It holds that
C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
consists of those
f\colon[0,T]\to\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that

\lVert f\rVert_{C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}<\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{r\to 0}\sup\biggl\{\frac{\lVert f(t)-f(s)\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}}{\lvert t-s\rvert^{\kappa}}:s\neq t\in[0,T],\penalty\ \lvert t-s\rvert<r\biggr\}=0

(A.6)

and each
C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is separable.

Proof.

We first prove that
C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is characterized by (
A.6
), that is, each
f\in C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
satisfies (
A.6
) and each
f
satisfying (
A.6
) lies in
C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

Assume
f\in C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, we need to show that (
A.6
) holds. It is clear that
\lVert f\rVert_{C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}<\infty
and it suffices to prove the second condition of (
A.6
). By definition, there exists a sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
f_{n}\in C^{\infty}_{T}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\lVert f_{n}-f\rVert_{C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}=0
. Let
r>0
and
s\neq t\in[0,T]
such that
\lvert t-s\rvert<r
, then

\frac{\lVert f(t)-f(s)\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}}{\lvert t-s\rvert^{\kappa}}\leq\lVert f-f_{n}\rVert_{C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}+\frac{\lVert f_{n}(t)-f_{n}(s)\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}}{\lvert t-s\rvert^{\kappa}},

which yields

\lim_{r\to 0}\sup\biggl\{\frac{\lVert f(t)-f(s)\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}}{\lvert t-s\rvert^{\kappa}}:s\neq t\in[0,T],\penalty\ \lvert t-s\rvert<r\biggr\}=\lVert f-f_{n}\rVert_{C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}.

Since
n\in\mathbb{N}
is arbitrary, we can pass to the limit to show that each
f\in C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
satisfies (
A.6
).

Next we need to show that each
f\colon[0,T]\to\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
satisfying (
A.6
) lies in
C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, i.e. that it can be approximated by a sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
f_{n}\in C_{T}^{\infty}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We extend
f
from
[0,T]
to
\mathbb{R}
by setting
f(t)=f(0)
for
t<0
and
f(t)=f(T)
for
t>T
. We define

\phi_{n}(r)\coloneq n\biggl(1-\frac{\lvert r\rvert^{2}}{n^{2}}\biggr)^{n^{4}}\pi^{-1/2}

and construct the polynomials

f_{n}(t)\coloneq\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(r)\phi_{n}(t-r)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mur=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t-r)\phi_{n}(r)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mur,

(A.7)

which converge to
f
in
C_{T}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
as
n\to\infty
, see
[
EK86
, Appendix, Prop. 7.1]
for the case of real-valued functions. The extension to Banach space-valued functions follows by the discussion in
[
AE09
, p. 177]
.

Next we show that
f_{n}\to f
in
C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, where we argue as in
[
Haj19
]
. Let
\varepsilon>0
be arbitrary, by the definition of
C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
we can find some
\tau>0
such that if
s\neq t\in[0,T]
and
\lvert t-s\rvert<\tau
, then

\lVert f(t)-f(s)\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}<\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon\lvert t-s\rvert^{\kappa},

(A.8)

which also extends from
[0,T]
to
\mathbb{R}
by our definition of
f
.
For every
n\geq T
it follows by
[
EK86
, Appendix, (7.3)]
that
\int_{[-T,T]}\lvert\phi_{n}(r)\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mur\leq 1
,
which combined with (
A.8
) and the definition of
f_{n}
yields

\displaystyle\lVert f_{n}(t)-f_{n}(s)\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}\leq\int_{[-T,T]}\lVert f(t-r)-f(s-r)\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}\lvert\phi_{n}(r)\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mur\leq\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon\lvert t-s\rvert^{\kappa}.

Consequently we obtain for all
\lvert t-s\rvert<\tau
, that

\lVert f(t)-f(s)-f_{n}(t)+f_{n}(s)\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}<\varepsilon\lvert t-s\rvert^{\kappa}.

Let
n
be sufficiently large such that
\lVert f-f_{n}\rVert_{C_{T}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}<\varepsilon\tau^{\kappa}/2
. Assume
\lvert t-s\rvert>\tau
, then

\lVert f(t)-f(s)-f_{n}(t)+f_{n}(s)\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}\leq 2\lVert f-f_{n}\rVert_{C_{T}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}<\varepsilon\tau^{\kappa}<\varepsilon\lvert t-s\rvert^{\kappa}.

This implies that for all
s\neq t\in[0,T]
and
n
sufficiently large,

\lVert(f-f_{n})(t)-(f-f_{n})(s)\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}<\varepsilon\lvert t-s\rvert^{\kappa}.

Since
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary, we obtain
\lim_{n\to\infty}\lVert f-f_{n}\rVert_{C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}=0
, which shows that each
f\colon[0,T]\to\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
satisfying (
A.6
) can be approximated by a polynomial. In particular,
f\in C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, which proves that (
A.6
) characterises the space
C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

To show that
C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is separable, it suffices to approximate every
f\in C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
by a polynomial with coefficients in a countable, dense subset of
\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, which exists by (
A.7
) and the separability of
\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
(Lemma
A.7
). This yields the claim.
∎

The next lemma presents a natural criterion that allows us to find elements of
C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

Lemma A.11
.

Let
T>0
,
p,q\in[1,\infty]
,
\alpha<\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
and
0\leq\kappa<\kappa^{\prime}<1
.

•

If
q\in[1,\infty)
, then each
f\colon[0,T]\to\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that
\lVert f\rVert_{C_{T}^{\kappa^{\prime}}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}<\infty
is an element of
C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

•

If
q=\infty
, then each
f\colon[0,T]\to\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that
\lVert f\rVert_{C_{T}^{\kappa^{\prime}}\mathcal{B}_{p,\infty}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}<\infty
is an element of
C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

Proof.

Let
T>0
,
p,q\in[1,\infty]
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
0\leq\kappa<\kappa^{\prime}<1
. To prove the first claim, let
q<\infty
and
f\colon[0,T]\to\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
be such that
\lVert f\rVert_{C_{T}^{\kappa^{\prime}}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}<\infty
. By Lemma
A.8
it follows that
f\colon[0,T]\to\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Let
r>0
and
s\neq t\in[0,T]
with
\lvert t-s\rvert<r
, we obtain

\frac{\lVert f(t)-f(s)\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}}{\lvert t-s\rvert^{\kappa}}\lesssim\lvert t-s\rvert^{\kappa^{\prime}-\kappa}\frac{\lVert f(t)-f(s)\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}}{\lvert t-s\rvert^{\kappa^{\prime}}}\leq r^{\kappa^{\prime}-\kappa}\lVert f\rVert_{C_{T}^{\kappa^{\prime}}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}}\to 0\quad\text{as}\penalty\ r\to 0,

hence
f
satisfies (
A.6
), which implies
f\in C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
by Lemma
A.11
. The second claim follows by the same argument, using that
\lVert f\rVert_{C_{T}^{\kappa^{\prime}}\mathcal{B}_{p,\infty}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}<\infty
implies
f\colon[0,T]\to\mathcal{B}_{p,\infty}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
by Lemma
A.8
.
∎

Recall the definition
\mathscr{L}_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\coloneq C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha-2\kappa}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\cap C_{T}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
(see Subsection
1.3
). Next we show that each
\mathscr{L}_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is separable.

Lemma A.12
.

Let
T>0
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
\kappa\in(0,1)
, then
\mathscr{L}_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})=C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha-2\kappa}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\cap C_{T}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is separable.

Proof.

The proof is classical: There exists an isometric isomorphism that maps
\mathscr{L}_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})=C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha-2\kappa}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\cap C_{T}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
to the diagonal
\{(f,f):f\in\mathscr{L}_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\}\subset C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha-2\kappa}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times C_{T}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. The product space
C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha-2\kappa}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times C_{T}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is a separable metric space by Lemma
A.10
, which implies that the diagonal is a separable subspace. Using that isometric isomorphisms preserve separability, it follows that each
\mathscr{L}_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{B}_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is separable.
∎

Let
F
be a separable, normed vector space and define
F^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
as in Subsection
1.3
. In the next lemma we show that
F^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
is separable.

Lemma A.13
.

Let
T>0
and
F
be a separable, normed vector space, then
F^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
is separable.

Proof.

The space
F^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
can be identified with a subspace of the metric space
F^{\textnormal{sol}}
defined in
[
Cha+22
, Sec. 1.5.1]
. As discussed in
[
Cha+22
, Sec. 1.5.1]
,
F^{\textnormal{sol}}
is a separable metric space, hence
F^{\textnormal{sol}}_{T}
is a separable subspace.
∎

Next we define the space of periodic space-time Hölder distributions (Definition
A.15
; see also Definition
A.17
for the analogous notion on the torus), which we use in Subsection
B.1
to quantify the regularity of space-time white noise (Theorem
B.2
).

Denote by
\mathfrak{s}=(\mathfrak{s}_{i})_{i=0}^{d}=(2,1,\ldots,1)
the parabolic scaling of
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
with norm
\lvert\mathfrak{s}\rvert=\sum_{i=0}^{d}\mathfrak{s}_{i}=2+d
.

Definition A.14

(
[
Hai14
, (2.10) & (2.13)]
)
.

Let
z=(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
z^{\prime}=(t^{\prime},x^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we define the parabolic distance

d_{\mathfrak{s}}(z,z^{\prime})\coloneq\lvert t-t^{\prime}\rvert^{1/\mathfrak{s}_{0}}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lvert x_{i}-x^{\prime}_{i}\rvert^{1/\mathfrak{s}_{i}}

and denote the parabolic unit ball by
B_{\mathfrak{s}}(0,1)\coloneq\{z\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}:\lvert z\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}<1\}
, where

\lvert z\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}\coloneq\lvert t\rvert^{1/\mathfrak{s}_{0}}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lvert x_{i}\rvert^{1/\mathfrak{s}_{i}}

(by a slight abuse of notation, since
\lvert\,\cdot\,\rvert_{\mathfrak{s}}
is not homogeneous, hence fails to be a norm.) We define the scaling of a function by
\lambda\in(0,1]
around the point
z
via

\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{s},z}^{\lambda}f(s,y)\coloneq\lambda^{-\lvert\mathfrak{s}\rvert}f(\lambda^{-\mathfrak{s}_{0}}(s-t),\lambda^{-\mathfrak{s}_{1}}(y_{1}-x_{1}),\ldots,\lambda^{-\mathfrak{s}_{d}}(y_{d}-x_{d})),\qquad f\colon\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}.

We can now define the space
\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{s},\textnormal{per}}^{\alpha}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
of periodic distributions of Hölder regularity
\alpha<0
.

Definition A.15
.

Let
\alpha<0
and
r_{\alpha}\coloneq\lceil-\alpha\rceil
, we define for each compact
\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
the seminorm

\lVert u\rVert_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha}(\mathfrak{K})}\coloneq\sup_{\lambda\in(0,1]}\sup_{\eta\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{s}}^{r_{\alpha}}}\sup_{z\in\mathfrak{K}}\frac{\lvert\langle u,\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{s},z}^{\lambda}\eta\rangle\rvert}{\lambda^{\alpha}},\qquad u\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}).

where

\mathcal{B}^{r_{\alpha}}_{\mathfrak{s}}\coloneq\{\eta\in C^{r_{\alpha}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}):\textnormal{supp}(\eta)\subset B_{\mathfrak{s}}(0,1),\penalty\ \lVert\eta\rVert_{C^{r_{\alpha}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 1\},

and

\lVert\eta\rVert_{C^{r_{\alpha}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}\coloneq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{1+d}\\
\lvert\nu\rvert\leq r_{\alpha}\end{subarray}}\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lvert\partial^{\nu}\eta(z)\rvert.

We define
\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{s},\textnormal{per}}^{\alpha}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\textnormal{per}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
as the completion of
C^{\infty}([0,T]\times[0,1]^{d})
under the norm
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}([0,T]\times[0,1]^{d})}
, where we extend each element of
C^{\infty}([0,T]\times[0,1]^{d})
to
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
periodically in space and by
0
in time.

Lemma A.16
.

For every
\alpha<0
it follows that the normed vector space
\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{s},\textnormal{per}}^{\alpha}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
is complete and separable.

Proof.

Completeness is clear since
\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{s},\textnormal{per}}^{\alpha}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined as a completion. To prove separability it suffices to show the existence of a countable subset of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{s},\textnormal{per}}^{\alpha}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
which can approximate every
u\in C^{\infty}([0,T]\times[0,1]^{d})
(extended to
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
periodically in space and by
0
in time) under the norm
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}([0,T]\times[0,1]^{d})}
.

An application of the Stone–Weierstrass theorem shows that for each
\varepsilon>0
there exists a polynomial
p\colon[0,T]\times[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\lVert u-p\rVert_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times[0,1]^{d})}\leq\varepsilon
. We extend this polynomial to
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
periodically in space and by
0
in time; in particular it still holds that
\lVert u-p\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\overline{\mathfrak{K}}_{1})}\leq\varepsilon
, where
\mathfrak{K}=[0,T]\times[0,1]^{d}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{K}}_{1}
denotes the
1
-fattening of
\mathfrak{K}
, that is, the closure of
\mathfrak{K}+B_{\mathfrak{s}}(0,1)
. We can now control the
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\mathfrak{K})
-norm of
u-p
by its
L^{\infty}(\overline{\mathfrak{K}}_{1})
-norm, since for every
\lambda\in(0,1]
,
\eta\in\mathcal{B}^{r_{\alpha}}_{\mathfrak{s}}
and
z\in\mathfrak{K}
,

\lvert\langle u-p,\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{s},z}^{\lambda}\eta\rangle\rvert\leq\lVert u-p\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\overline{\mathfrak{K}}_{1})}\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\lVert u-p\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\overline{\mathfrak{K}}_{1})}\leq\varepsilon,

which implies

\lVert u-p\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\mathfrak{K})}=\sup_{\lambda\in(0,1]}\sup_{\eta\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{s}}^{r_{\alpha}}}\sup_{z\in\mathfrak{K}}\frac{\lvert\langle u-p,\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{s},z}^{\lambda}\eta\rangle\rvert}{\lambda^{\alpha}}\lesssim\varepsilon,

showing that the (suitably extended) polynomials are dense in
C^{\infty}([0,T]\times[0,1]^{d})
under the norm
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\mathfrak{K})}
. A similar argument then implies that the (suitably extended) polynomials with rational coefficients are dense as well, which yields the separability of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{s},\textnormal{per}}^{\alpha}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
∎

We define the space
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
as those distributions in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
, whose periodization lies in
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s},\textnormal{per}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Definition A.17
.

Let
\Gamma\colon\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\textnormal{per}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a linear homeomorphism (which exists by Lemma
A.19
below.) We define for every
\alpha<0
,

\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})\coloneq\{u\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d}):\Gamma(u)\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{s},\textnormal{per}}^{\alpha}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\}

and equip
\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
with the natural norm that turns
\Gamma
into a linear isometric isomorphism.

Lemma A.18
.

For every
\alpha<0
it follows that
\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d})
is a separable Banach space.

Proof.

Completeness and separability are preserved under isometric isomorphisms; hence the claim follows by Lemma
A.16
.
∎

The following lemma is classical, here we follow the presentation contained in the unpublished notes
[
vZui22
]
.

Lemma A.19

(
[
vZui22
, Lem. 31.3]
)
.

Let
\Psi\colon\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to C^{\infty}_{\textnormal{per}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be given by

\Psi(\phi)\coloneq\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\mathcal{T}_{z}\phi,\qquad\mathcal{T}_{z}\phi(x)\coloneq\phi(x-z)

and
\gamma\colon C^{\infty}_{\textnormal{per}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
be the linear homeomorphism

\gamma(\phi)([x])\coloneq\phi(x),\qquad[x]\coloneq x+\mathbb{Z}^{d}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}.

Then
\Gamma\colon\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\textnormal{per}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
given by

\langle\Gamma(u),\phi\rangle=\langle u,\gamma(\Psi(\phi))\rangle

is a linear homeomorphism with inverse

\Gamma^{-1}\colon\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\textnormal{per}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),\qquad\langle\Gamma^{-1}(u),\phi\rangle=\langle u,\chi\gamma^{-1}(\phi)\rangle,

where
\chi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is such that
(\mathcal{T}_{z}\chi)_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
forms a partition of unity (which exists by
[
vZui22
, Lem. 29.3]
.) The above extends to
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\textnormal{per}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{d})

mutatis mutandis
.

Appendix B
Space-Time White Noise

In this appendix we show the existence and regularity of space-time white noise (Theorem
B.2
), construct an abstract Wiener space (Theorem
B.3
) and show that iterated Itô integrals as in (
1.12
) are elements of inhomogeneous Wiener chaoses (Lemma
B.5
).

B.1
Existence and Regularity

We first define space-time white noise as a family of Gaussian random variables.

Definition B.1

(
[
Hai14
, Sec. 10.1]
)
.

We define a space-time white noise on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
to be a family of real-valued, centred Gaussian random variables
\{\xi(\phi):\phi\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})\}
on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, such that
\mathbb{E}[\xi(\phi)\xi(\psi)]=\langle\phi,\psi\rangle_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})}
for every
\phi,\psi\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})
.

By an application of Kolmogorov’s extension theorem, there exists a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
and a family as in Definition
B.1
. Indeed, following
[
Jan97
, Ex. 1.27]
, we may take
\Omega
to be the algebraic dual to
L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
, define for all
h\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
the functions
\xi_{h}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
,
\omega\mapsto\xi_{h}(\omega)\coloneq\omega(h)
, and set
\mathcal{F}=\sigma(\xi_{h}:h\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}))
. Then there exists a unique probability measure
\mathbb{P}
on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
such that each
\xi_{h}
is centred Gaussian with variance
\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})}^{2}
.

Next we show that there exists a modification of this family that is a random distribution in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})
and that this modification lies in the space
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})
(see Definition
A.17
).

Theorem B.2
.

A space-time white noise on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
as defined in Definition
B.1
exists as a random distribution in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})
and is almost surely an element of
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})
for each
\alpha<-2
.

Proof.

Following
[
Hai14
, below Rem. 10.1]
, let us consider the periodically-extended noise

\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})\ni\phi\mapsto\Gamma(\xi\mathds{1}_{[0,T]})(\phi),

where
\Gamma
denotes the homeomorphism constructed in Lemma
A.19
. For each
\alpha<-2
it follows by
[
Hai14
, Lem. 10.2]
that
\Gamma(\xi\mathds{1}_{[0,T]})
is almost surely a random variable in
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s},\textnormal{per}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})
. In particular, by Definition
A.17
we obtain almost surely
\xi\mathds{1}_{[0,T]}\in\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{s}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})
.
∎

Given two independent space-time white noises
\xi^{1}
and
\xi^{2}
as in Definition
B.1
on the same probability space, we can define a vector-valued space-time white noise
\bm{\xi}=(\xi^{1},\xi^{2})
by

\bm{\xi}(\phi)\coloneq(\xi^{1}(\phi),\xi^{2}(\phi)),\qquad\phi\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}).

With this definition it is clear how to pass from scalar-valued to vector-valued noise. In the remainder of this appendix, for the sake of notation, we only consider scalar-valued noise and omit the target space from our definitions.

B.2
Abstract Wiener Space

In the following we do not distinguish between
L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
and the functions in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
that are zero on the complement of
[0,T]
.

Theorem B.3
.

For every
\alpha<-2
it follows that the law of the space-time white noise on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
can be realized on the abstract Wiener space
(B,\mathcal{H}_{\mu},\mu)
with separable Banach space
B\coloneq\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
, Cameron–Martin space
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}=L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
and Gaussian probability measure
\mu=\textnormal{Law}(\xi)
.

Proof.

It follows by Theorem
B.2
that
\mu
is a Borel probability measure on
B
. Hence to prove that
\mu
is a Gaussian probability measure in the sense of
[
Led96
, Sec. 4]
, we need to show that the law of each continuous linear functional on
B
is Gaussian.

It follows by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and the assumption
\alpha<-2
, that
L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})\subset\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})=B
,
which implies by standard arguments
B^{\prime}\subset L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
.
Consequently we obtain by Definition
B.1
that for each
l\in B^{\prime}
,
l_{\#}\mu
is a centred Gaussian measure on
\mathbb{R}
, which implies that
\mu
is a Borel probability measure on
B
.

It remains to identify the Cameron–Martin space
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}
. To do so, we first consider the reproducing kernel Hilbert space
\mathcal{R}_{\mu}
, i.e. the closure of
B^{\prime}
in
L^{2}(B,\mu)
, which is isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}
. Using a duality argument, we obtain
C_{\textnormal{c}}^{\infty}((0,T)\times\mathbb{T}^{2})\subset B^{\prime}
(where we extend each element of
C_{\textnormal{c}}^{\infty}((0,T)\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
by
0
to
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
).
The closure of
B^{\prime}
in
L^{2}(B,\mu)
coincides with the closure of
B^{\prime}
in
L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
since for all
l\in B^{\prime}\subset L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
it holds that
\lVert l\rVert_{L^{2}(B,\mu)}=\lVert l\rVert_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}
. Hence, using that
C_{\textnormal{c}}^{\infty}((0,T)\times\mathbb{T}^{2})\subset B^{\prime}
, we obtain
\mathcal{R}_{\mu}=L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
.

By
[
Led96
, Sec. 4]
there exists a linear isometric isomorphism

\iota\colon\mathcal{R}_{\mu}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mu},\qquad\iota(l)\coloneq\int_{B}xl(x)\mu(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mux),

so that it suffices to identify each element
\iota(l)\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu}
with an element of
L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
. Let
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
, we obtain

\langle\iota(l),\phi\rangle=\int_{B}\phi(x)l(x)\mu(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mux)=\langle l,\phi\rangle_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})},

which shows that
\iota(l)
coincides with the tempered distribution

u_{l}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}),\qquad\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})\ni\phi\mapsto u_{l}(\phi)=\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}l(z)\phi(z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muz.

Using that the map
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})\ni l\mapsto u_{l}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
is an embedding,
we can identify
\iota(l)
and
l
, which yields
\mathcal{H}_{\mu}=L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
.
∎

B.3
Iterated Itô Integrals

In this subsection we relate the iterated Itô integrals defined in (
1.12
) to our space-time white noise (Lemma
B.4
), show that each integral yields an element of a homogenous Wiener chaos (Lemma
B.5
) and characterize its homogeneous part (Lemma
B.6
).

Lemma B.4
.

Let
\bm{\xi}
be a vector-valued space-time white noise on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
as in Subsection
B.1
. Then there exists a family of complex-valued Brownian motions
(W^{j}(\,\cdot\,,\omega))_{\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},j=1,2}
(on the same underlying probability space) that satisfy
\overline{W^{j}(\,\cdot\,,\omega)}=W^{j}(\,\cdot\,,-\omega)
but are otherwise independent.

Proof.

Let
\bm{\xi}
be a vector-valued space-time white noise on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
as in Subsection
B.1
. Let
t\in[0,T]
,
\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
j=1,2
and define

B^{j}(t,\omega)\coloneq\bm{\xi}(\mathds{1}_{[0,t]}(\,\cdot\,)\otimes\cos(2\pi\langle\omega,\,\cdot\,\rangle)\otimes\delta_{j,\,\cdot\,}),\quad\tilde{B}^{j}(t,\omega)\coloneq\bm{\xi}(\mathds{1}_{[0,t]}(\,\cdot\,)\otimes-\sin(2\pi\langle\omega,\,\cdot\,\rangle)\otimes\delta_{j,\,\cdot\,}),

which yields a family of Brownian motions such that
\mathbb{E}[(B^{j}(t,\omega))^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[(\tilde{B}^{j}(t,\omega))^{2}]=t/2
,
B^{j}(t,\omega)=B^{j}(t,-\omega)
and
\tilde{B}^{j}(t,\omega)=-\tilde{B}^{j}(t,-\omega)
and which are otherwise independent. Using this family, we can define the complex Brownian motion
W^{j}(t,\omega)\coloneq B^{j}(t,\omega)+\mathrm{i}\mkern 0.7mu\tilde{B}^{j}(t,\omega)
.
∎

Let
\Sigma_{n}(1,\ldots,n)
denote the permutation group over
\{1,\ldots,n\}
. We call
h\in L^{2}([0,T]^{n}\times\mathbb{T}^{2n};\mathbb{R}^{2n})
symmetric, if

h(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=\Sym(h)(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\varsigma\in\Sigma_{n}(1,\ldots,n)}h(u_{\varsigma(1)},x_{\varsigma(1)},j_{\varsigma(1)},\ldots,u_{\varsigma(n)},x_{\varsigma(n)},j_{\varsigma(n)}),

where for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
we denote
u_{k}\in[0,T]
,
x_{k}\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
,
j_{k}\in\{1,2\}
and
z_{k}=(u_{k},x_{k},j_{k})
. We denote the space of symmetric, square-integrable functions by
f\in L^{2}_{\textnormal{sym}}([0,T]^{n}\times\mathbb{T}^{2n};\mathbb{R}^{2n})
.
Next we show that each iterated Itô integrals of a symmetric integrand is an element of a homogeneous Wiener chaos as defined in (
C.1
).

Lemma B.5
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
f\in L^{2}_{\textnormal{sym}}([0,T]^{n}\times\mathbb{T}^{2n};\mathbb{R}^{2n})
, it holds that
I^{n}(f)\in\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(\Omega,\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R})
, where the homogeneous Wiener chaos
\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(\Omega,\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R})
is defined as in (
C.1
).

Proof.

Let
f\in L^{2}_{\textnormal{sym}}([0,T]^{n}\times\mathbb{T}^{2n};\mathbb{R}^{2n})
, it is now a consequence of
[
Nua06
, below Thm. 1.1.2]
that
I^{n}(f)\in\mathcal{H}^{(n)}(\Omega,\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R})
.
∎

Using that each iterated Itô integral has zero mean, we obtain the following result (cf.
[
HW15
, Sec. 4]
).

Lemma B.6
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
f\in L^{2}_{\textnormal{sym}}([0,T]^{n}\times\mathbb{T}^{2n};\mathbb{R}^{2n})
and
h\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Then it follows that
n!\mathbb{E}[I^{n}(f)(\,\cdot\,+h)]=\langle f,h^{\otimes n}\rangle_{L^{2}([0,T]^{n}\times\mathbb{T}^{2n};\mathbb{R}^{2n})}
.

Sketch of Proof.

For every
h\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
we can apply Lemma
B.4
to identify the shifted vector-valued space-time white noise
\bm{\xi}+h
with a complex Brownian motion plus drift, that is, for every
t\in[0,T]
,
\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
j=1,2
,

(\bm{\xi}+h)(\mathds{1}_{[0,t](\,\cdot\,)}\otimes\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 0.7mu\langle\omega,\,\cdot\,\rangle}\otimes\delta_{j,\,\cdot\,})=W^{j}(t,\omega)+\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{h}(s,\omega,j)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mus.

In the case of iterated Itô integrals of order
1
it follows that for every integrand
f\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[I^{1}(f)(\,\cdot\,+h)]&=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{\omega_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\widehat{f}(u_{1},-\omega_{1},j_{1})(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muW^{j_{1}}(u_{1},\omega_{1})+\widehat{h}(u_{1},\omega_{1},j_{1})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muu_{1})\Bigr]\\
&=\sum_{\omega_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\widehat{f}(u_{1},-\omega_{1},j_{1})\widehat{h}(u_{1},\omega_{1},j_{1})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muu_{1}\\
&=\langle f,h\rangle_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})},\end{split}

where in the second equality we used that Itô integrals have zero mean and in the third equality applied Parseval’s theorem.

The case of general
n\in\mathbb{N}
follows by linearity, where we use the symmetry of
f
to recover the integral over
[0,T]^{n}\times\mathbb{T}^{2n}\times\{1,2\}^{n}
modulo a factor of
n!
. For the full proof, see
[
Klo17
, Prop. 1.33]
.
∎

Appendix C
Generalized Freidlin–Wentzell Theory

In this appendix we generalize Freidlin and Wentzell’s theory to (direct sums of) random variables taking values in Banach space-valued inhomogeneous Wiener chaoses (Theorem
C.4
).

A similar program has already been carried out in
[
HW15
, Sec. 3]
and the main observation of the present appendix is that the results of
[
HW15
, Sec. 3]
carry over to situations in which the notion of convergence
[
HW15
, (3.9)]
is replaced by (
C.4
) (where one retains powers of the noise intensity for lower-order Wiener chaoses), if one assumes that the limit has no contributions from lower-order Wiener chaoses (
C.5
). It is this change that allows us to apply Theorem
C.4
directly to the (canonical) enhancement constructed in Theorem
4.1
, rather than going through a perturbation argument as in
[
HW15
, Thm. 4.7]
, which does not generically apply for us as we wish to treat cases where the white-noise induced renormalisation is strictly a distribution rather than a smooth function (or as in
[
HW15
]
a constant.)

Let
(B,\mathcal{H}_{\mu},\mu)
be an abstract Wiener space where
B
denotes a real, separable Banach space and
\mu
a centred Gaussian probability measure on
B
with Cameron–Martin space
(\mathcal{H}_{\mu},\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}})
. Let
\mathcal{R}_{\mu}
be the associated reproducing kernel Hilbert space, i.e. the completion of the dual space
B^{\prime}
in
L^{2}(B,\mu)
. Let
(e_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be an orthonormal basis in
\mathcal{R}_{\mu}
such that
e_{k}\in B^{\prime}
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, which exists by an application of the Gram–Schmidt procedure.

We define the Hermite polynomials
H_{n}(x)
, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, through the series expansion

\lambda\mapsto\exp\Bigl(\lambda x-\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}\Bigr)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda^{n}}{\sqrt{n!}}H_{n}(x)

and the generalized Hermite polynomials
H_{\nu}(\bm{x})
, for every multi-index
\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{N}}
with finitely-many non-zero entries and
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
, by

H_{\nu}(\bm{x})\coloneq\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}H_{\nu_{i}}(x_{i}),

which we extend to Banach space-valued arguments
\xi\in B
via

H_{\nu}(\xi)\coloneq H_{\nu}((\langle\xi,e_{i}\rangle)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}).

Let
E
be a real, separable Banach space, we denote by
L^{2}(B,\mu;E)
the space of functions
\Psi\colon B\to E
that are Bochner measurable and square integrable under
\mu
. We can now define the
E
-valued homogeneous Wiener chaos of degree
k\in\mathbb{N}
, denoted by
\mathcal{H}^{(k)}(B,\mu;E)
, as in
[
HW15
, (3.3)]
:

\mathcal{H}^{(k)}(B,\mu;E)\coloneq\Bigl\{\Psi\in L^{2}(B,\mu;E):\int_{B}\Psi(\xi)H_{\nu}(\xi)\mu(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mu\xi)=0\penalty\ \text{for all}\penalty\ \lvert\nu\rvert\neq k\Bigr\},

(C.1)

where the absolute value of the multi-index
\nu
is given by
\lvert\nu\rvert\coloneq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\nu_{i}
. We further set
\mathcal{H}^{(0)}(B,\mu;E)\coloneq E
.

Definition C.1
.

Let
\mathcal{W}
be a finite index set. For each
\tau\in\mathcal{W}
, let
K_{\tau}
be a natural number,
E_{\tau}
be a real, separable Banach space and
\Psi_{\tau}
be an element of the
E_{\tau}
-valued inhomogeneous Wiener chaos of order
K_{\tau}
, i.e.

\Psi_{\tau}\coloneq\sum_{k=0}^{K_{\tau}}\Psi_{\tau,k},\qquad\Psi_{\tau,k}\in\mathcal{H}^{(k)}(B,\mu;E_{\tau}).

Denote
\mathbf{E}=\bigoplus_{\tau\in\mathcal{W}}E_{\tau}
and let
\bm{\Psi}
be given by

\bm{\Psi}\coloneq\bigoplus_{\tau\in\mathcal{W}}\Psi_{\tau}=\bigoplus_{\tau\in\mathcal{W}}\sum_{k=0}^{K_{\tau}}\Psi_{\tau,k},\qquad\Psi_{\tau,k}\in\mathcal{H}^{(k)}(B,\mu;E_{\tau}).

(C.2)

Given
\varepsilon>0
and a random variable
\bm{\Psi}
of the form (
C.2
), we define the rescaling

\bm{\Psi}^{(\varepsilon)}\coloneq\bigoplus_{\tau\in\mathcal{W}}\varepsilon^{\frac{K_{\tau}}{2}}\Psi_{\tau}.

Definition C.2
.

Let
\bm{\Psi}
be a random variable of the form (
C.2
),
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\delta(\varepsilon)=0
and
(\bm{\Psi}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a sequence such that

\bm{\Psi}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}=\bigoplus_{\tau\in\mathcal{W}}\varepsilon^{\frac{K_{\tau}}{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{K_{\tau}}\Psi_{\delta(\varepsilon);\tau,k},\qquad\Psi_{\delta(\varepsilon);\tau,k}\in\mathcal{H}^{(k)}(B,\mu;E_{\tau}).

(C.3)

We say that
\bm{\Psi}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}
converges to
\bm{\Psi}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
if for each
\tau\in\mathcal{W}
and
k\leq K_{\tau}
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{K_{\tau}-k}\int_{B}\lVert\Psi_{\delta(\varepsilon);\tau,k}(\xi)-\Psi_{\tau,k}(\xi)\rVert_{E_{\tau}}^{2}\mu(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mu\xi)=0.

(C.4)

Definition C.3
.

Let
\bm{\Psi}
be a random variable of the form (
C.2
). We define the homogeneous part
\bm{\Psi}_{\hom}\colon\mathcal{H}_{\mu}\to\mathbf{E}
of
\bm{\Psi}
by

\bm{\Psi}_{\hom}\coloneq\bigoplus_{\tau\in\mathcal{W}}(\Psi_{\tau,K_{\tau}})_{\hom},

where for each
\tau\in\mathcal{W}
and
h\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu}
,

(\Psi_{\tau,K_{\tau}})_{\hom}(h)\coloneq\int_{B}\Psi_{\tau,K_{\tau}}(\xi+h)\mu(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mu\xi).

The homogeneous part
(\Psi_{\tau,K_{\tau}})_{\hom}(h)
is well-defined by the Cameron–Martin theorem
[
Led96
, (4.11)]
and
[
HW15
, (3.8)]
.

We can now state a large deviation principle for random variables of the form (
C.3
) that converge in the sense of (
C.4
) to a limit that does not have any contributions from lower-order Wiener chaoses.

Theorem C.4
.

Let
(\bm{\Psi}^{(\varepsilon)}_{\delta(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a family of random variables of the form (
C.3
) converging in the sense of (
C.4
) to a random variable
\bm{\Psi}
such that

\bm{\Psi}=\bigoplus_{\tau\in\mathcal{W}}\Psi_{\tau,K_{\tau}},\qquad\Psi_{\tau,K_{\tau}}\in\mathcal{H}^{(K_{\tau})}(B,\mu;E_{\tau}).

(C.5)

It then follows that
(\bm{\Psi}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}^{(\varepsilon)})_{\varepsilon>0}
satisfies a large deviation principle in
\mathbf{E}
with speed
\varepsilon
and good rate function
\mathscr{I}_{\bm{\Psi}}
given by

\mathscr{I}_{\bm{\Psi}}(\bm{s})\coloneq\inf\Bigl\{\frac{1}{2}\lVert h\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}}^{2}:h\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu}\penalty\ \text{with}\penalty\ \bm{\Psi}_{\hom}(h)=\bm{s}\Bigr\}.

Proof.

The key observation is that the proof of
[
HW15
, Lem. 3.7]
carries over if we assume (
C.4
) and (
C.5
) instead of
[
HW15
, (3.9)]
. One can then adapt the strategy of
[
HW15
, Thm. 3.5]
to our setting.
∎

Appendix D
The Enhancement Driven by a Cameron–Martin Element

In this appendix we analyse the regularity of the mild solution
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}=\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}h]
driven by a Cameron–Martin element
h\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
(Lemmas
D.1
&
D.2
), which we then use to construct the enhancement
\mathbb{X}^{h}=(\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h},\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h},\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h},\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h})
(cf. (
D.3
)) appearing in Theorem
4.7
(Lemma
D.3
).

For every
t\in[0,T]
and
\omega=(\omega^{1},\omega^{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, we can represent the Fourier transform of
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}
by

\widehat{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}}(t,\omega)=\sum_{j=1}^{2}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}H_{t-u}^{j}(\omega)\widehat{\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}}(u,\omega-m)\widehat{h}(u,m,j)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muu,

(D.1)

where
H^{j}_{t-u}(\omega)=2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 0.7mu\omega^{j}\exp(-\lvert t-u\rvert\lvert 2\pi\omega\rvert^{2})
(see Subsection
1.3
).

The next lemma establishes the regularity of
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}
as a (Hölder-)continuous function in time.

Lemma D.1
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}\geq 0
and
\overline{\rho_{0}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
2.1
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence (see Lemma
2.4
). Then for all
T<T^{*}
,
h\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\kappa\in[0,1/2]
it holds that

\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}\rVert_{C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{H}^{-2\kappa}}\lesssim\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\rVert_{C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})}

and in particular
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}\in C_{T}L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
.

Proof.

An application of Lemma
2.6
yields
\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
; in particular
\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}h\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and upon redefining
h
, we may assume that
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}=\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[h]
.

Let
\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
s,t\in[0,T]
with
s<t
. We bound by Jensen’s inequality and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

\begin{split}\lvert\widehat{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}}(t,\omega)-\widehat{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}}(s,\omega)\rvert^{2}&\lesssim\sum_{j=1}^{2}\Bigl\lvert\int_{0}^{s}(H_{t-u}^{j}(\omega)-H_{s-u}^{j}(\omega))\widehat{h}(u,\omega,j)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muu\Bigr\rvert^{2}+\Bigl\lvert\int_{s}^{t}H_{t-u}^{j}(\omega)\widehat{h}(u,\omega,j)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muu\Bigr\rvert^{2}\\
&\leq\sum_{j=1}^{2}\Bigl(\int_{0}^{s}\lvert H_{t-u}^{j}(\omega)-H_{s-u}^{j}(\omega)\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muu\Bigr)\Bigl(\int_{0}^{s}\lvert\widehat{h}(u,\omega,j)\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muu\Bigr)\\
&\hskip 30.00005pt\ignorespaces+\Bigl(\int_{s}^{t}\lvert H_{t-u}^{j}(\omega)\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muu\Bigr)\Bigl(\int_{s}^{t}\lvert\widehat{h}(u,\omega,j)\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muu\Bigr).\end{split}

It follows by interpolation that for all
\kappa\in[0,1/2]
,

\lvert\widehat{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}}(t,\omega)-\widehat{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}}(s,\omega)\rvert^{2}\lesssim\lvert t-s\rvert^{2\kappa}\lvert\omega\rvert^{4\kappa}\lVert\widehat{h}(\omega)\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{2})},

(D.2)

which allows us to estimate by (
1.10
),

\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}_{t}-\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{-2\kappa}}^{2}=\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}(1+\lvert 2\pi\omega\rvert^{2})^{-2\kappa}\lvert\widehat{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}}(t,\omega)-\widehat{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}}(s,\omega)\rvert^{2}\lesssim\lvert t-s\rvert^{2\kappa}\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}.

By the definition of the
C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{H}^{-2\kappa}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
-norm, we obtain the bound

\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}\rVert_{C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{H}^{-2\kappa}}\lesssim\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

To establish
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}\in C_{T}L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, we approximate and use the completeness of
C_{T}L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
under the supremum-norm. Let
(h_{\delta})_{\delta>0}
be a sequence such that
h_{\delta}\in L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2}))
for every
\delta>0
and
\gamma\geq 0
, and such that
h_{\delta}\to h\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
as
\delta\to 0
.
An application of (
D.2
) yields for all
s,t\in[0,T]
with
s<t
,

\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h_{\delta}}_{t}-\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h_{\delta}}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{\gamma-2\kappa}}^{2}\lesssim\lvert t-s\rvert^{2\kappa}\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}(1+\lvert 2\pi\omega\rvert^{2})^{\gamma}\lVert\widehat{h_{\delta}}(\omega)\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{2})}=\lvert t-s\rvert^{2\kappa}\lVert h_{\delta}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2}))},

hence
\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h_{\delta}}\rVert_{C_{T}^{\kappa}\mathcal{H}^{\gamma-2\kappa}}<\infty
, which, upon choosing
\gamma\geq 2\kappa
, implies
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h_{\delta}}\in C_{T}L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
. Another application of (
D.2
) allows us to deduce

\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}-\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h_{\delta}}\rVert_{C_{T}L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\lVert h-h_{\delta}\rVert_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\to 0\quad\text{as}\penalty\ \delta\to 0

and using that
C_{T}L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
is complete we obtain
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}\in C_{T}L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, which yields the claim.
∎

The next lemma establishes the regularity of
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}
as a square-integrable function in time.

Lemma D.2
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}\geq 0
and
\overline{\rho_{0}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
2.1
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence (see Lemma
2.4
). Then for all
T<T^{*}
and
h\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
it holds that

\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}\rVert_{L^{2}_{T}\mathcal{H}^{1}}\lesssim\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\rVert_{C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

and in particular
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}\in L^{2}_{T}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
D.1
, we may assume that
\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}=\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[h]
. Using (
D.1
), we decompose

\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}\rVert_{L^{2}_{T}\mathcal{H}^{1}}^{2}=\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{0\}}(1+\lvert 2\pi\omega\rvert^{2})\Bigl\lvert\sum_{j=1}^{2}2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 0.7mu\omega^{j}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-\lvert t-u\rvert\lvert 2\pi\omega\rvert^{2}}\widehat{h}(u,\omega,j)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muu\Bigr\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mut.

We then apply the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality followed by Young’s convolution inequality to estimate

\begin{split}\lVert\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}\rVert_{L^{2}_{T}\mathcal{H}^{1}}^{2}&\leq\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{0\}}(1+\lvert 2\pi\omega\rvert^{2})\lvert 2\pi\omega\rvert^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\Bigl\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-\lvert t-u\rvert\lvert 2\pi\omega\rvert^{2}}\widehat{h}(u,\omega,j)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5muu\Bigr\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mut\\
&\leq\sum_{j=1}^{2}\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{0\}}(1+\lvert 2\pi\omega\rvert^{2})\lvert 2\pi\omega\rvert^{2}\Bigl(\int_{0}^{T}\mathrm{e}\mkern 0.7mu^{-t\lvert 2\pi\omega\rvert^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mut\Bigr)^{2}\Bigl(\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\widehat{h}(t,\omega,j)\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mut\Bigr)\\
&\lesssim\sum_{j=1}^{2}\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\widehat{h}(t,\omega,j)\rvert^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mkern 0.5mut=\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2},\end{split}

which yields the claim.
∎

Let
\mathbb{X}^{h}\coloneq(\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h},\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h},\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h},\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h})
be the enhancement driven by the Cameron–Martin element
h\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, i.e.

\begin{split}&\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}\coloneq\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}h],\quad\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}\coloneq\nabla\cdot\mathcal{I}[\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}\nabla\Phi_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}}],\\
&\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}\coloneq\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}\mathbin{\varodot}\nabla\Phi_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}}+\nabla\Phi_{\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h}}\mathbin{\varodot}\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h},\quad\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scal (D.3)

We can construct
\mathbb{X}^{h}
by combining Lemma
D.1
, Schauder’s estimate
[
MM25
, Lem. A.6]
and Bony’s estimate.

Lemma D.3
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be such that
\rho_{0}\geq 0
and
\overline{\rho_{0}}=1
,
\rho_{\textnormal{det}}
be the weak solution to (
2.1
) with initial data
\rho_{0}
and chemotactic sensitivity
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T^{*}
be its maximal time of existence (see Lemma
2.4
). Then for all
T<T^{*}
,
h\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\alpha<-2
and
\kappa\in(0,1/2)
, it holds that
\mathbb{X}^{h}=(\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={32},width=2.84544pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h},\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={33},width=7.11317pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h},\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={34},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h},\mathchoice{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}{\scalebox{0.8}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}{\scalebox{0.64}{\,\includegraphics[page={35},width=10.243pt]{diagrams_coloured.pdf}\,}}^{h})\in\mathcal{X}^{\alpha,\kappa}_{T}
is well-defined as a distribution and

\lVert\mathbb{X}^{h}\rVert_{\mathcal{X}^{\alpha,\kappa}_{T}}\lesssim\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\rVert_{C_{T}L^{\infty}}\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})}(1\vee\lVert\sqrt{\rho_{\textnormal{det}}}\rVert^{2}_{C_{T}L^{\infty}}\lVert h\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})}).
